- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-099.csv`
- Pitfalls: HAS-BLED still embedded in local templates → replace, do not add, or both get skipped.; Scores done on apps but not written → count only documented scores; say so at teaching.; Clinicians fear ORBIT will deny anticoagulation → stress it guides risk discussion, not a cut-off.; Starts in clinics missed → limit to inpatient starts for cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the anticoagulation pharmacist; they review every new start already.; Put CHA2DS2-VASc and ORBIT side by side on the same form.; Pre-fill ORBIT items from the lab to cut effort.; Ask the cardiology lead to email the change to all medical registrars.

**NNA-100. What proportion of adults with atrial fibrillation on the medical wards are prescribed aspirin alone for stroke prevention?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG196 rec 1.6.14 (2021, updated June 2021): "Do not offer aspirin monotherapy solely for stroke prevention to people with atrial fibrillation. [2014]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng196/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No aspirin monotherapy for AF stroke prevention at discharge. Aspirin for another documented indication (e.g. recent ACS, stent) counts as a pass.. Target: 0% aspirin monotherapy for AF (≥95% pass). Sample: 50 consecutive adults with AF discharged from medical wards over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Add a pharmacist discharge check: any AF patient on aspirin alone generates an 'indication?' query to the team via the EPR discharge medicines screen.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-100.csv`
- Pitfalls: Aspirin started years ago in primary care → count it and give the GP a clear recommendation in the discharge letter.; Other antiplatelet indications missed → check cardiology history and stents before failing a case.; Frail patients' 'no anticoagulant' decisions labelled as fails → record documented decisions separately.; Pharmacist capacity → run the check only for AF discharges flagged by the EPR.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward pharmacist as co-lead.; Use the EPR problem list to find AF patients quickly.; Give a one-line script for the discharge letter: 'Aspirin stopped; anticoagulation discussed'.; Report strokes avoided using simple CHA2DS2-VASc rates to engage managers.

**NNA-101. What proportion of adults discharged after a heart failure admission with reduced ejection fraction are on all four NICE-recommended drug classes, or have a documented reason for any omission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG106 rec 1.4.1 (2018, updated September 2025): "Offer an angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitor, a beta-blocker, a mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist (MRA) and a sodium-glucose cotransporter-2 (SGLT2) inhibitor to people with heart failure with reduced ejection fraction. [2025]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng106/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Discharged on an ACE inhibitor (or ARNI/ARB per NG106 1.4.3–1.4.4), beta-blocker, MRA and SGLT2 inhibitor, with each missing class having a documented reason (e.g. hyperkalaemia, eGFR below licence, hypotension, intolerance).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive HFrEF discharges over the last 3 months (use the NHFA case list).
- Change: Add a 'four pillars' checklist with a mandatory reason box for each omitted class to the heart failure discharge template in the EPR.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-101.csv`
- Pitfalls: LVEF missing or old → use the most recent echo within 12 months and record the date.; Drugs held for acute kidney injury never restarted → count a documented 'restart plan' as a reason.; Cardiology and general medicine patients differ → report the two groups separately.; Checklist ticked without reasons → make the reason box mandatory for any 'no'.
- Pearls: Recruit the heart failure specialist nurses; they hold the NHFA list.; Pre-fill the checklist from the discharge prescription so it takes seconds.; Ask the pharmacist to flag missing SGLT2 inhibitors on the discharge screen.; Add titration instructions for the GP to the same template.

**NNA-102. What proportion of adults discharged after an NSTEMI have their left ventricular function result written in the discharge summary to the GP?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG185 recs 1.2.27 and 1.2.29 (2020): "1.2.27 Assess left ventricular function in all people who have had an NSTEMI. [2013] 1.2.29 Record measures of left ventricular function in the person's care record and in correspondence with the primary healthcare team and the person. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng185/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: LV function assessed before discharge (echo, cardiac MRI or LV angiogram) and the result (EF or grade) stated in the discharge summary.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive NSTEMI discharges over the last 3 months (use the MINAP case list).
- Change: Add a mandatory 'LV function (EF %/grade and method)' field to the ACS discharge summary template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-102.csv`
- Pitfalls: Echo done after discharge → record the date and count only pre-discharge assessment for the first part.; Result in the cath report but not the letter → the pass needs the letter, as the GP relies on it.; Type 2 MI coded as NSTEMI → use MINAP type 1 NSTEMI only.; Mandatory field filled with 'see notes' → define acceptable entries.
- Pearls: Recruit the MINAP audit clerk to supply cases.; Ask echo to prioritise inpatient ACS scans before discharge.; Auto-pull the latest EF from the echo system into the template if possible.; Report the number of low-EF patients who missed MRA or device review.

**NNA-103. What proportion of nebuliser prescriptions for adults admitted with a COPD exacerbation state the driving gas?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG115 recs 1.3.11–1.3.12 (2018, updated 2019): "If a person with COPD is hypercapnic or acidotic the nebuliser should be driven by compressed air rather than oxygen (to avoid worsening hypercapnia). If oxygen therapy is needed, administer it simultaneously by nasal cannulae. [2004] 1.3.12 The driving gas for nebulised therapy should always be specified in the prescription. [2004]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng115/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Every nebuliser prescription in the first 48 hours names the driving gas (air or oxygen); for patients with hypercapnia (PaCO2 >6 kPa) the gas is air.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive COPD exacerbation admissions over the last 6–8 weeks.
- Change: Make 'driving gas: air / oxygen' a mandatory field on nebuliser prescriptions in e-prescribing (or add it to the pre-printed chart), defaulting to air when a target of 88–92% is prescribed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-103.csv`
- Pitfalls: Ward has no medical air outlets → check supply with estates first; portable air compressors may be needed.; Nurses choose the gas regardless of prescription → include nursing staff in the teaching and record the gas used where charted.; Blood gas not done → record hypercapnia as unknown and report separately.; IT change delayed → use a chart sticker as an interim fix.
- Pearls: Recruit a respiratory nurse specialist and the e-prescribing pharmacist early.; Build the default from the prescribed oxygen target range to cut clicks.; Put 'Air-driven nebs' signs by COPD beds.; Share results through the NACAP quarterly report.

**NNA-104. What proportion of adults admitted with a COPD exacerbation are prescribed oral prednisolone 30 mg daily for 5 days?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG115 recs 1.3.13 and 1.3.16 (2018, updated 2019): "1.3.13 In the absence of significant contraindications, use oral corticosteroids, in conjunction with other therapies, in all people admitted to hospital with a COPD exacerbation. [2004] 1.3.16 Offer 30 mg oral prednisolone daily for 5 days. [2019]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng115/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Prednisolone 30 mg once daily prescribed with a total course of 5 days (inpatient plus discharge doses), or a documented reason for a different course.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive COPD exacerbation discharges over the last 6–8 weeks.
- Change: Add a 'COPD exacerbation' prednisolone order set in e-prescribing: 30 mg once daily with an automatic stop date at day 5, and a prompt to count days already given.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-104.csv`
- Pitfalls: Doses given before admission not counted → record GP or ambulance rescue-pack doses.; Asthma overlap cases prescribed longer courses → exclude documented overlap under specialist care.; Discharge letter restarts a fresh 5 days → check the discharge prescription carefully.; Order set bypassed via free text → review free-text steroid prescriptions in cycle 2.
- Pearls: Recruit the COPD nurse specialist who already runs the discharge bundle.; Print 'Day x of 5' on the drug chart line.; Ask the pharmacist to reconcile steroid days on discharge.; Report cumulative steroid exposure avoided.

**NNA-105. What proportion of adult inpatients scored on NEWS2 SpO2 Scale 2 meet the RCP criteria for using it?** (new)
- Standard: Royal College of Physicians, National Early Warning Score (NEWS) 2 (2017), recommendations 25–26: "25 For patients confirmed to have hypercapnic respiratory failure on blood gas analysis on either a prior or their current hospital admission, and requiring supplemental oxygen, we recommend (i) a prescribed oxygen saturation target range of 88–92%, and (ii) that the dedicated SpO2 scoring scale (Scale 2) on the NEWS2 chart should be used to record and score the oxygen saturation for the NEWS. 26 The decision to use SpO2 scale 2 should be made by a competent clinical decision maker and should be recorded in the patient's clinical notes." https://www.rcp.ac.uk/media/a4ibkkbf/news2-final-report_0_0.pdf
- Pass: Scale 2 in use AND a documented clinician decision in the notes AND a blood gas (this or a previous admission) showing hypercapnic respiratory failure.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: Point-prevalence: all Scale 2 patients across medical wards on 3–4 audit days (aim 30–50 patients).
- Change: Configure the e-observations system so Scale 2 can only be switched on after a clinician enters the date of the qualifying blood gas and their name; auto-expire at discharge.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-105.csv`
- Pitfalls: Previous-admission gases hard to find → search the blood gas archive by hospital number before cycle 1.; Nurses start Scale 2 from habit → include nursing leads in the change.; Scale 2 carried over between admissions → record whether it was re-confirmed.; IT lock not feasible → use a mandatory note template linked to the Scale 2 option.
- Pearls: Recruit the outreach team lead; they see missed deteriorations.; Pull the Scale 2 list directly from the e-observations system.; Report any case where Scale 1 would have triggered a higher response.; Give registrars a one-line rule: 'No gas, no Scale 2'.

**NNA-106. What proportion of adults treated for community-acquired pneumonia have antibiotics stopped at 5 days, or a documented reason to continue?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG250 rec 1.6.3 (2025): "For adults with community-acquired pneumonia, stop antibiotic treatment after 5 days unless: microbiological results suggest a longer course is needed or the person is not clinically stable, for example, if they have had a fever in the past 48 hours or have more than 1 of the following signs of clinical instability: systolic blood pressure less than 90 mmHg; heart rate more than 100 beats per minute; respiratory rate more than 24 breaths per minute; oxygen saturations of less than 90% on room air (or failure to meet long-term baseline oxygen requirements) [2019, amended 2025]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng250/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Total antibiotic course (IV plus oral, inpatient plus discharge) is 5 days, or a longer course has a documented reason matching the NICE exceptions.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive CAP discharges over the last 6 weeks.
- Change: Set a default 5-day stop date on CAP antibiotics in e-prescribing (IV and oral share one counter), with a 'continue because...' option at day 5.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-106.csv`
- Pitfalls: Discharge supplies add days → always count discharge prescriptions.; IV-to-oral switch restarts the clock → make the counter shared.; Hospital-acquired pneumonia mixed in → confirm onset within 48 hours of admission.; Clinicians fear under-treatment → share the NICE instability criteria as the only reasons to extend.
- Pearls: Recruit the antimicrobial pharmacist as co-lead.; Report antibiotic days saved per 40 patients.; Add the 5-day rule to the post-take ward round template.; Share results with GPs, as many courses end in the community.

**NNA-107. What proportion of adult inpatients with confirmed C. difficile infection have their proton pump inhibitor reviewed within 48 hours of the result?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG199 rec 1.1.4 (2021): "For people with suspected or confirmed C. difficile infection, review the need to continue any treatment with: proton pump inhibitors; other medicines with gastrointestinal activity or adverse effects, such as laxatives; medicines that may cause problems if people are dehydrated, such as non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors, angiotensin‑2 receptor antagonists and diuretics." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng199/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Within 48 hours of the positive toxin result, the PPI is stopped, reduced, or continued with a documented indication.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All toxin-positive inpatients on a PPI over the last 6–12 months (aim 30–40; use the infection control list).
- Change: Add a PPI, laxative and antibiotic review box to the C. difficile care bundle sticker/EPR form completed at the infection control review.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-107.csv`
- Pitfalls: Low case numbers → use a 12-month window or pool with a neighbouring trust.; Genuine PPI indications failed → accept a documented indication (e.g. Barrett's, recent GI bleed).; PPI restarted at discharge → check the discharge prescription.; Infection team and ward team each assume the other acts → name the ward pharmacist as owner.
- Pearls: Recruit the infection prevention nurse who reviews every case.; Add the PPI question to the existing root-cause analysis form.; Ask the pharmacist to flag PPIs when toxin results come back.; Give GPs a clear stop or review instruction.

**NNA-108. What proportion of adults with a first unprovoked DVT or PE have no extra cancer imaging unless they have symptoms or signs of cancer?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 recs 1.8.1–1.8.2 (2020, updated 2023): "1.8.1 For people with unprovoked DVT or PE who are not known to have cancer, review the medical history and baseline blood test results including full blood count, renal and hepatic function, PT and APTT, and offer a physical examination. [2020] 1.8.2 Do not offer further investigations for cancer to people with unprovoked DVT or PE unless they have relevant clinical symptoms or signs (for further information, see the NICE guideline on suspected cancer). [2020]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: History, examination and baseline bloods documented, and no CT abdomen/pelvis, CT chest (beyond the diagnostic CTPA), tumour markers or other cancer imaging unless a symptom or sign is documented first.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive unprovoked VTE diagnoses over the last 3 months.
- Change: Replace the 'screen for malignancy' line in the local unprovoked VTE pathway/clinic proforma with the NICE check (history, examination, bloods; imaging only for symptoms or signs).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-108.csv`
- Pitfalls: 'Unprovoked' labelled inconsistently → define it before cycle 1 (no major transient risk factor in the preceding 3 months).; Imaging requested by GPs later → check order comms for 3 months after diagnosis.; Symptoms documented after the scan → require documentation before the request.; Clinicians worry about missed cancer → report 12-month cancer outcomes alongside.
- Pearls: Recruit the thrombosis nurse who runs the DVT clinic.; Ask radiology to vet 'occult malignancy' CT requests against the pathway.; Report avoided scans and cost to managers.; Keep the change within the existing clinic proforma.

**NNA-109. What proportion of adults with confirmed pulmonary embolism have a validated risk score documented before the decision to admit or treat as an outpatient?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG158 rec 1.2.1 (2020, updated 2023): "Consider outpatient treatment for suspected or confirmed low-risk PE, using a validated risk stratification tool to determine the suitability of outpatient treatment. [2020]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng158/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: PESI, sPESI or Hestia score documented in the ED/AMU notes before the admission or discharge decision.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive confirmed PEs from ED/AMU over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory sPESI (or Hestia) box to the ED/AMU PE management proforma, with the ambulatory pathway linked for low scores.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-109.csv`
- Pitfalls: Score calculated but not written → count only documented scores.; No ambulatory pathway exists → agree follow-up capacity with the ambulatory unit first.; Low-risk patients admitted for social reasons → record the reason.; Small numbers of outpatients → report readmissions carefully with raw numbers.
- Pearls: Recruit an ambulatory care consultant.; Link the sPESI box to the ambulatory referral in one click.; Give patients the NG158 contact details sheet.; Report bed-days saved to operational managers.

**NNA-110. What proportion of adults admitted with a gout flare who have CKD stage 3–5 or take a diuretic leave hospital with urate-lowering therapy started or planned?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG219 recs 1.5.1 and 1.5.4 (2022): "1.5.1 Offer ULT, using a treat-to-target strategy, to people with gout who have: multiple or troublesome flares; CKD stages 3 to 5 (glomerular filtration rate [GFR] categories G3 to G5); diuretic therapy; tophi; chronic gouty arthritis. 1.5.4 Start ULT at least 2 to 4 weeks after a gout flare has settled. If flares are more frequent, ULT can be started during a flare (see the section on preventing flares when starting or titrating ULT)." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng219/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Discharge summary records ULT started, continued, or a plan for the GP to start ULT 2–4 weeks after the flare with a urate target (<360 µmol/L), or a documented decline.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive inpatients with gout flare over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add a 'gout flare' discharge template paragraph (ULT indication, start timing, urate target, recheck in 4 weeks) triggered when colchicine is prescribed for gout.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-110.csv`
- Pitfalls: Gout flares poorly coded → find cases through colchicine prescriptions.; ULT stopped during the flare → record and teach that ULT continues.; GPs do not act on the plan → put a clear action and date in the summary.; Few cases → extend the window or add orthopaedic and surgical wards.
- Pearls: Recruit a rheumatology registrar as co-lead.; Use the pharmacy system to find colchicine starts.; Suggest a nurse-led gout clinic referral where available.; Report readmissions with gout as a secondary outcome.

**NNA-111. What proportion of adults with active rheumatoid arthritis have a DAS28-CRP score recorded every month until remission or low disease activity?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG100 rec 1.2.3 (2018, updated 2020): "In adults with active RA, measure C-reactive protein (CRP) and disease activity (using a composite score such as DAS28) monthly in specialist care until the target of remission or low disease activity is achieved. [2018]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng100/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: In the first 6 months of active disease (DAS28 >3.2), CRP and DAS28 recorded at least every 5 weeks until DAS28 ≤3.2.. Target: ≥75%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients with active RA from clinic lists over the last 9–12 months (follow each for up to 6 months).
- Change: Book a 4-weekly nurse-led DAS28 telephone or face-to-face slot automatically when DAS28 is >3.2, with a CRP request form sent at the same time.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-111.csv`
- Pitfalls: Follow-up takes 6 months → start cycle 1 retrospectively and accept a 3-month window in cycle 2.; Joint counts done but DAS28 not calculated → count only documented scores.; Clinic capacity limits → use nurse telephone reviews with patient-reported joint counts where validated locally.; Definition of 'monthly' varies → fix the 5-week tolerance before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the rheumatology specialist nurse team early; they will deliver the slots.; Use NEIAA data to find new active cases.; Put DAS28 in the letter header so it is easy to find.; Track time to remission as the headline outcome.

**NNA-112. What proportion of adult medical inpatients with a positive blood culture have their antibiotic reviewed within 24 hours of the sensitivity result?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG253 rec 1.9.1 (2025, updated 2026): "When the source of infection is confirmed or microbiological results are available: review the choice of antibiotic(s) and change the antibiotic(s) according to results, using a narrower-spectrum antibiotic, if appropriate. [2024]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng253/chapter/Antibiotic-therapy-intravenous-fluid-and-oxygen
- Pass: Within 24 hours of the sensitivity report, a documented review with the antibiotic narrowed, changed to match sensitivities, or continued with a stated reason.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive significant positive blood cultures over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'blood culture result reviewed: action' task in the EPR that fires to the responsible team when sensitivities are released.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-112.csv`
- Pitfalls: Microbiology phones results but notes are blank → accept a documented phone call with action.; Contaminants inflate numbers → use microbiology's significance call.; Weekend results wait until Monday → report weekday and weekend separately.; Task ignored → route it to the ward pharmacist as well.
- Pearls: Recruit a microbiology consultant and the antimicrobial pharmacist.; Pull report times straight from the lab system.; Present bug–drug mismatches as safety events.; Pair with an IV-to-oral switch prompt.

**NNA-113. What proportion of adult smokers admitted to medical wards who accept nicotine replacement therapy have it prescribed within 12 hours of admission?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG209 recs 1.14.10 and 1.14.15 (2021, updated 2025): "1.14.10 Offer and arrange or supply prescriptions of stop-smoking options (see the sections on stop-smoking interventions and stop-smoking pharmacotherapies in acute and mental health services). [2013, amended 2021] 1.14.15 If stop-smoking pharmacotherapy is accepted, make sure it is provided immediately. [2013]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng209/chapter/Treating-tobacco-dependence
- Pass: Smoking status recorded at clerking and, for smokers not declining, NRT prescribed within 12 hours of arrival with a first dose administered.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive smokers admitted via AMU over the last 3–4 weeks.
- Change: Add an opt-out NRT order set to the AMU clerking template: smokers get combination NRT pre-selected unless the clerking doctor declines it.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-113.csv`
- Pitfalls: Smoking status missing → count missing status as a fail and report it separately.; Patients vaping instead → record e-cigarette use and do not fail if the patient declines NRT.; NRT prescribed but not stocked on the ward → check ward stock lists first.; Opt-out order set seen as coercive → keep a one-click decline.
- Pearls: Recruit the tobacco dependence (CURE-type) team; they gain referrals.; Stock combination NRT on AMU.; Tell patients about ward vaping and smoking rules at clerking.; Report self-discharge rates among smokers as an outcome.

**NNA-114. What proportion of adults with diabetes admitted to medical wards have a documented foot risk assessment within 24 hours of admission?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG19 recs 1.3.3–1.3.4 (2015, updated 2019): "1.3.3 For adults with diabetes, assess their risk of developing a diabetic foot problem at the following times: When diabetes is diagnosed, and at least annually thereafter (see the recommendation on carrying out reassessments at intervals, depending on the person's risk of developing a diabetic foot problem). If any foot problems arise. On any admission to hospital, and if there is any change in their status while they are in hospital. [2015] 1.3.4 When examining the feet of a person with diabetes, remove their shoes, socks, bandages and dressings, and examine both feet for evidence of the following risk factors: neuropathy (use a 10 g monofilament as part of a foot sensory examination); limb ischaemia (see the NICE guideline on peripheral arterial disease); ulceration; callus; infection and/or inflammation; deformity; gangrene" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng19/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Both feet examined with a risk category (low/moderate/high/active) documented within 24 hours of admission; moderate/high risk patients have heel offloading documented.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults with diabetes admitted to medical wards over the last 3–4 weeks.
- Change: Add the NaDIA-style foot risk check (both feet, monofilament, pulses, risk category) as a mandatory item in the nursing admission assessment for patients with a diabetes flag, linked to heel offloading.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-114.csv`
- Pitfalls: Foot check documented as 'feet OK' without detail → require a risk category.; Monofilaments not available → check ward stock before cycle 1.; Nurses and doctors each assume the other did it → name the nursing admission assessment as the place.; Gains fade as staff rotate → add the item to the nursing admission audit run monthly.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes inpatient specialist nurse and a podiatrist.; Keep monofilaments on each ward's observation trolley.; Link 'moderate/high risk' to automatic heel offloading and podiatry referral.; Report hospital-acquired foot lesions as the outcome that managers watch.

**NNA-115. What proportion of adults treated for diabetic ketoacidosis have their usual long-acting insulin continued, or started if newly diagnosed, while on the fixed-rate insulin infusion?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS-IP 02 The Management of Diabetic Ketoacidosis in Adults (revised March 2023), p15 and key recommendations: "In those already on long acting basal insulin, it should continue to be prescribed at their usual dose. In those newly diagnosed, then a long acting basal insulin should be commenced, at a dose of 0.25 units/Kg subcutaneously once daily. ... 4. Subcutaneous long-acting analogue/human insulin should be continued" https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_02_DKA_Guideline_with_QR_code_March_2023.pdf
- Pass: Long-acting insulin prescribed at the usual dose (or 0.25 units/kg if new) and given at the next due time while the FRIII runs, with no missed dose.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive DKA episodes over the last 6–12 months.
- Change: Build the basal insulin line into the DKA e-prescribing order set as a required field ('continue usual basal: name/dose' or '0.25 units/kg if new').
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-115.csv`
- Pitfalls: DKA episodes are few → use a 12-month window and prospective capture.; Pump users counted wrongly → exclude or record separately.; Basal dose prescribed but omitted by nursing → record administration, not just prescription.; ED starts DKA before the ward → check ED drug charts too.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes inpatient specialist nurse; they see every DKA.; Put the basal line at the top of the DKA order set.; Report rebound ketosis or readmission as outcomes.; Add a 'basal insulin given?' question to the DKA nursing care plan.

**NNA-116. What proportion of adult inpatients with a capillary glucose below 4.0 mmol/L have a repeat glucose check within 15 minutes of treatment?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS-IP 01 The Hospital Management of Hypoglycaemia in Adults with Diabetes Mellitus (revised January 2023), p11 step 4: "4. Repeat capillary blood glucose measurement 10-15 minutes later. If it is still less than 4.0mmol/L, repeat step 3 (no more than 3 treatments in total). If it is above 4.0mmol/L then go to step 7." https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_01_Hypo_Guideline_with_QR_code_January_2023.pdf
- Pass: A repeat capillary glucose recorded within 20 minutes of the first low reading (15 minutes after treatment, plus 5 minutes allowed because treatment time is rarely recorded).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive hypoglycaemia episodes (one per patient) over the last 2–4 weeks.
- Change: Configure networked glucose meters to prompt 'Recheck in 10–15 minutes' after any reading <4.0 mmol/L, and place hypo boxes with a timer card on each ward.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-116.csv`
- Pitfalls: Meters not networked → use paper glucose charts and accept lower precision.; Reading repeated on another meter → search by patient, not meter.; Nurses treat but record late → allow a 5-minute tolerance and state it.; Prompt ignored → pair with hypo box refresh and ward feedback.
- Pearls: Recruit the point-of-care testing manager; they can export meter data in minutes.; Recruit a ward nurse champion on each ward.; Report recurrent hypos within 24 hours as the outcome.; Share weekly ward-level recheck rates on the diabetes dashboard.

**NNA-117. What proportion of adult inpatients with known diabetes started on glucocorticoids have capillary glucose checked four times a day?** (new)
- Standard: JBDS-IP 08 Management of Hyperglycaemia and Steroid (Glucocorticoid) Therapy (revised January 2023), Table 1, p9: "Known diagnosis of diabetes: Reassess glucose control and current therapy. Set target blood glucose e.g. 6-10mmol/L. Check CBG 4 times a day and use this flowchart to adjust diabetes medication accordingly. In Type 1 diabetes also check daily for ketones if CBG >12mmol/L. Aim for 6-10mmol/L (acceptable range 6-12mmol/L)." https://abcd.care/sites/default/files/site_uploads/JBDS_Guidelines_Current/JBDS_08_Management_of_Hyperglycaemia_and_Steroid_%28Glucocorticoid%29_Therapy_with_QR_code_January_2023.pdf
- Pass: At least 4 capillary glucose readings on each of the first 3 full days of steroid therapy (or until discharge), and diabetes treatment adjusted if CBG >12 mmol/L twice in 24 hours.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible inpatients over the last 4–6 weeks.
- Change: Link a steroid prescription in a patient with a diabetes flag to an automatic 'CBG four times daily' monitoring order and a pop-up link to the JBDS steroid flowchart.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-117.csv`
- Pitfalls: Once-daily morning steroid peaks late → check that the afternoon/evening readings are included.; Meter data not linked to patients → use paper charts for cycle 1.; Short courses (<3 days) → audit days on steroid only.; Pop-up fatigue → make it a monitoring order, not an alert.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes specialist nurse and the e-prescribing pharmacist.; Use the COPD and oncology wards, where volume is high.; Teach the 'afternoon peak' in one slide.; Report hyperglycaemia >12 mmol/L days as the outcome.

**NNA-118. What proportion of adults treated with insulin-glucose for hyperkalaemia have capillary glucose monitored for at least 6 hours afterwards?** (new)
- Standard: UK Kidney Association (Renal Association) Clinical Practice Guideline: Treatment of Acute Hyperkalaemia in Adults (2020, v2 July 2022), Guideline 17.2: "We recommend that the blood glucose concentration is monitored at regular intervals (0, 15, 30, 60, 90, 120, 180, 240, 360, 480 and 720 minutes) up to 12 hours after administration of insulin-glucose infusion in all patients with hyperkalaemia. (1C)" https://www.ukkidney.org/sites/default/files/RENAL%20ASSOCIATION%20HYPERKALAEMIA%20GUIDELINE%20-%20JULY%202022%20V2_0.pdf
- Pass: Glucose recorded before treatment and at least at 30, 60, 120, 240 and 360 minutes (±15 minutes) after insulin-glucose. Full compliance to 12 hours reported as a secondary measure.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive insulin-glucose treatments over the last 6–8 weeks.
- Change: Add a hyperkalaemia insulin-glucose order set that schedules glucose checks (0, 30, 60, 120, 240, 360 min, then to 12 h) as timed nursing tasks and adds 10% glucose if pre-treatment glucose is <7 mmol/L.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-118.csv`
- Pitfalls: Readings spread across blood gases and meters → search both sources.; Patients move from ED to AMU mid-monitoring → follow the patient, not the ward.; Strict time windows fail good care → state the ±15 minute tolerance before cycle 1.; Order set used but tasks not done → record completed readings, not scheduled ones.
- Pearls: Recruit the renal pharmacist and an ED nurse lead.; Put a printed timer card in the hyperkalaemia treatment pack.; Report hypoglycaemia rates to motivate staff.; Align with the local hyperkalaemia guideline so there is one document.

## Older people, stroke, neurology and palliative care

**NNA-119. Do adults with a suspected TIA receive aspirin 300 mg at first contact in the emergency department or acute medical unit?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.1.4 (2019, updated 2022): "Offer aspirin (300 mg daily), unless contraindicated, to people who have had a suspected TIA, to be started immediately." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Aspirin 300 mg (or a documented alternative antiplatelet if allergic or intolerant) given before leaving ED/AMU or within 4 hours of first hospital assessment, or a contraindication documented.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults referred to the TIA clinic from ED/AMU over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'aspirin 300 mg given or contraindication' tick box to the electronic TIA clinic referral form, so the referral cannot be sent without it.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-119.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients referred straight from GP never pass through ED → limit the sample to ED/AMU referrals and state it.; 'Prescribed' confused with 'given' → count only a signed administration entry.; Patients already on aspirin 75 mg are marked as passes → agree before cycle 1 that a 300 mg loading dose is still expected unless the specialist says otherwise.; Stroke mimic diagnosed later → keep them in; the standard applies to suspected TIA.
- Pearls: Recruit the TIA clinic nurse; they already see every referral and can flag missing doses daily.; Agree the 4-hour window with the stroke lead before cycle 1 and keep it fixed.; Ask the EPR team for a report of TIA referrals with aspirin administration times to cut collection time.; Put the result on the ED safety huddle board for a month after the change.

**NNA-120. How many adults with a suspected TIA have a CT brain scan without a documented suspicion of an alternative diagnosis?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.2.1 (2019, updated 2022): "Do not offer CT brain scanning to people with a suspected TIA unless there is clinical suspicion of an alternative diagnosis that CT could detect." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Either no CT brain before specialist assessment, or a CT request that states a specific alternative diagnosis CT could detect (for example subdural haematoma, tumour, head injury).. Target: ≥90% of CT scans justified. Sample: 40 consecutive adults with suspected TIA over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'alternative diagnosis suspected' drop-down to the CT head request for suspected TIA in the radiology ordering system, with a note that routine CT is not needed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-120.csv`
- Pitfalls: ED doctors worry about missing a bleed → agree the list of valid alternative diagnoses with the stroke lead and radiology before cycle 1.; Vague request text ('?CVA') counted as justified → define in advance that a named alternative diagnosis is required.; Radiologists vet differently between cycles → keep the same vetting rules across both cycles.; Posters alone fade → rely on the ordering-system field.
- Pearls: Recruit a radiology registrar; they can pull request text from RIS in one search.; Report the number of avoidable scans and radiation dose saved; managers respond to this.; Pair the change with fast-track MRI after clinic so staff feel imaging is not being withheld.; Add the rule to the junior doctor induction handbook for the next rotation.

**NNA-121. Do adults with a warfarin-related intracerebral haemorrhage receive both prothrombin complex concentrate and intravenous vitamin K?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.4.16 (2008, amended 2019): "Return clotting levels to normal as soon as possible in people with a primary intracerebral haemorrhage who were receiving warfarin before their stroke (and have elevated international normalised ratio). Do this by reversing the effects of the warfarin using a combination of prothrombin complex concentrate and intravenous vitamin K." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Both PCC and intravenous vitamin K given, with PCC given within 90 minutes of the diagnostic CT (local timing standard), unless a documented decision for palliation only.. Target: 100% receive both agents; ≥80% PCC within 90 minutes. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 24 months (expect 15–30 in a district general hospital), or SSNAP extract of ICH on anticoagulants.
- Change: Store a pre-labelled 'ICH warfarin reversal pack' (PCC dosing chart, vitamin K 10 mg IV, INR request) in ED resus, triggered by the stroke CT result.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-121.csv`
- Pitfalls: Very small numbers → use a 24-month look-back and present counts, not percentages alone.; PCC given but not recorded in EPMA → cross-check the blood bank issue log.; Haematology and ED disagree on PCC dose → agree the dosing chart with haematology before the change.; Patients moved to a neurosurgical centre → collect data from the transfer letter and the receiving hospital.
- Pearls: Ask the transfusion practitioner to join; they own the PCC issue log.; Agree the 90-minute local timing standard with the stroke lead before cycle 1.; Add the reversal pack check to the monthly ED resus trolley checklist.; Extend to DOAC reversal once the warfarin loop is closed.

**NNA-122. In adults with acute intracerebral haemorrhage eligible for rapid blood pressure lowering, is systolic pressure brought to 140 mmHg or lower without a fall of more than 60 mmHg in the first hour?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.5.6 (2022): "When rapidly lowering blood pressure in people with acute intracerebral haemorrhage, aim to reach a systolic blood pressure of 140 mmHg or lower while ensuring that the magnitude drop does not exceed 60 mmHg within 1 hour of starting treatment." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Systolic BP 140 mmHg or lower reached during the first 6 hours of treatment, with no reading in the first hour of treatment more than 60 mmHg below the pre-treatment systolic.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: All eligible patients over the last 12 months (expect 30–40), or SSNAP extract.
- Change: Introduce a one-page ICH BP chart with 15-minute BP boxes for the first hour, the 140 mmHg target and a 'stop if drop over 60 mmHg' line, filed with the ED stroke proforma.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-122.csv`
- Pitfalls: Observations recorded hourly, not every 15 minutes → use the bespoke chart and accept only timed readings.; Eligibility argued case by case → agree the exclusion list (NICE rec 1.5.7) with the stroke consultant before cycle 1.; Small monthly numbers → use a 12-month look-back and report counts.; Nurses unsure about infusion titration → add a titration table to the chart, reviewed by pharmacy.
- Pearls: Recruit a stroke specialist nurse to keep the chart stocked in ED resus.; Use SSNAP's onset and arrival times so both cycles use identical definitions.; Report both efficacy (target) and safety (drop) so critics of over-treatment are reassured.; Hand over the chart and a named owner to the next registrar at rotation.

**NNA-123. Do adults admitted with acute stroke receive supplemental oxygen only when their oxygen saturation is below 95%?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.5.1 (2008): "Give supplemental oxygen to people who have had a stroke only if their oxygen saturation drops below 95%. The routine use of supplemental oxygen is not recommended in people with acute stroke who are not hypoxic." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: In the first 72 hours, either no supplemental oxygen, or oxygen started only after a documented saturation below 95% and stopped once saturation is 95% or above on air (or at a documented alternative target for chronic lung disease).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive stroke admissions over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a stroke-specific oxygen line to the stroke admission order set: 'Oxygen only if SpO2 below 95%; review every shift'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-123.csv`
- Pitfalls: Oxygen given by paramedics continues by habit → record the start time and whether hospital staff reviewed it.; Pulse oximeter over-reads in dark skin (NICE note) → collect ethnicity where recorded and discuss with the team.; Unrecorded oxygen use → check both the observation chart and the prescription.; Staff fear under-treating → share the NICE wording at the start.
- Pearls: Recruit a stroke nurse champion for the daily check.; Agree the handling of chronic lung disease targets before cycle 1.; Use the electronic observation system report to pull all oxygen flags in minutes.; Link the result to aspiration pneumonia and mobility, which the team already cares about.

**NNA-124. Do adults with acute stroke have all capillary blood glucose readings between 4 and 11 mmol/L in the first 72 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.5.2 (2008): "Maintain a blood glucose concentration between 4 and 11 mmol/litre in people with acute stroke." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: All recorded capillary or laboratory glucose values in the first 72 hours are 4–11 mmol/L, or every out-of-range value has a documented action within 1 hour.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive stroke admissions over the last 2 months (report diabetes and non-diabetes separately).
- Change: Add a stroke glucose protocol to the stroke admission order set: 4-hourly capillary glucose for 72 hours with a linked correction and hypoglycaemia plan.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-124.csv`
- Pitfalls: Non-diabetic patients have too few readings to judge → record the number of readings and report patients with fewer than 3 separately.; Point-of-care readings not linked to EPR → ask the POCT lead for a download.; Diabetes team pushback on insulin workload → agree the correction plan with them before the change.; Hypoglycaemia from over-correction → count readings below 4 as fails.
- Pearls: Recruit the diabetes inpatient specialist nurse early.; Fix the 72-hour window and 1-hour action rule before cycle 1.; Use the POCT system to identify out-of-range values quickly.; Keep a named nurse owner of the protocol on the stroke unit.

**NNA-125. Do adults with acute stroke who cannot take adequate nutrition orally have a nasogastric tube placed within 24 hours of admission?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.6.4 (2008, amended 2019): "People with acute stroke who are unable to take adequate nutrition, fluids and medication orally should: receive tube feeding with a nasogastric tube within 24 hours of admission unless they have had thrombolysis" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Nasogastric tube placed and position confirmed within 24 hours of admission (or within 24 hours of thrombolysis where the local protocol delays it), or a documented decision not to tube feed (for example end-of-life care or patient refusal).. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible stroke admissions over the last 4 months.
- Change: Add an automatic 24-hour 'NG decision due' prompt to the stroke admission proforma for any patient who fails the swallow screen, with the decision (tube or reason not) recorded on the board round sheet.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-125.csv`
- Pitfalls: Patients who pass a modified-texture diet are counted → define 'unable to take adequate nutrition' before cycle 1.; Families decline tubes → count a documented decision as a pass.; Out-of-hours staff unable to place tubes → collect time of day and report weekend and night delays separately.; NG tube safety never events → never shortcut position checks to meet the target.
- Pearls: Recruit the stroke dietitian and a nurse trained in NG placement.; Record weekend versus weekday separately; it usually explains most delays.; Add 'NG decision' as a fixed line on the stroke board round.; Share results with the nutrition steering group to keep support after rotation.

**NNA-126. Do adults with acute stroke who were taking a statin before admission have it continued without a missed dose?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG128 rec 1.4.22 (2008): "Continue statin treatment in people with acute stroke who are already receiving statins." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng128/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Pre-admission statin prescribed within 24 hours of admission and no doses omitted in the first 7 days except for a documented clinical reason; for nil-by-mouth patients, given via enteral tube or a documented plan.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible stroke admissions over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add statin to the stroke admission order set 'continue if on pre-admission' section, with a crushable or liquid option linked for nil-by-mouth patients.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-126.csv`
- Pitfalls: Statins held deliberately for haemorrhage → count a documented reason as a pass.; Omission codes misused → cross-check with notes for 5 random cases.; Confusion with rec 1.4.21 (no immediate new start) → the audit covers only patients already on a statin.; Pharmacy reconciliation delayed → use the GP record where reconciliation is missing.
- Pearls: Partner with the stroke ward pharmacist; they already review omissions.; Use the EPMA omitted-dose report to cut collection time.; Agree the handling of intensity changes (for example switch to atorvastatin 80 mg) before cycle 1.; Include the result in the monthly medicines safety report so it survives rotation.

**NNA-127. Are adults seen in the emergency department after a first suspected seizure seen by a first-seizure specialist within 2 weeks?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 1.1.1 (2022, updated 2026): "Refer children, young people and adults urgently (for an appointment within 2 weeks) for an assessment after a first suspected seizure: For adults, refer to a clinician with expertise in assessing first seizures and diagnosing epilepsy." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/1-Diagnosis-and-assessment-of-epilepsy
- Pass: Appointment with a first-seizure clinic or neurologist attended (or offered and declined) within 14 days of the ED attendance.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults over the last 3–4 months.
- Change: Create a single electronic 'first seizure' referral in the ED discharge system that books straight into ring-fenced first-seizure clinic slots.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-127.csv`
- Pitfalls: Coding misses seizures recorded as 'collapse' → search both codes and screen notes.; Neurology capacity is the real barrier → present the gap as slots needed per week, not blame.; Patients referred to GP instead → count as not referred.; Changing the definition of first seizure between cycles → fix the exclusion list before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit a neurologist or epilepsy nurse who runs the clinic.; Count slots needed per week from cycle 1 data and ask for exactly that.; Add DVLA advice as a second field; it is often missing and easy to fix together.; Make the ED discharge letter auto-copy the first-seizure clinic.

**NNA-128. Do adults attending hospital after a first suspected seizure have a 12-lead ECG recorded?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 1.2.2 (2022, updated 2026): "Evaluate people after a first suspected seizure with a 12-lead ECG to help identify cardiac-related conditions that could mimic an epileptic seizure." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/1-Diagnosis-and-assessment-of-epilepsy
- Pass: 12-lead ECG recorded and reviewed (QTc or interpretation documented) during the ED or AMU attendance.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add '12-lead ECG' as an auto-requested item in the ED 'seizure' triage order set so it is done at triage.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-128.csv`
- Pitfalls: ECG done but not reviewed → require a documented interpretation.; ECG stored only on paper → check the ECG system and scanned notes.; Triage nurses overloaded → agree with the nurse lead before adding to triage.; Small numbers of abnormal ECGs → report them as cases, not rates.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED triage nurse lead.; Pull the ECG system's timestamp list to save time.; Combine with the first-seizure referral audit for one data collection.; Add the triage order set to the ED induction slides.

**NNA-129. How many adults with established epilepsy attending the emergency department after a typical seizure have a CT head without a documented other concern?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 1.3.7 (2022, updated 2026): "Do not carry out a CT scan for people with established epilepsy presenting at an emergency department after a typical seizure, unless there are other concerns." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/1-Diagnosis-and-assessment-of-epilepsy
- Pass: No CT head, or a CT request documenting a specific other concern (for example head injury meeting NICE head injury criteria, new focal deficit, atypical seizure, anticoagulation, prolonged post-ictal state).. Target: ≥90% of CTs justified. Sample: 40 consecutive attendances over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add an 'established epilepsy, typical seizure' option in the CT head request form that requires selection of a specific other concern to proceed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-129.csv`
- Pitfalls: 'Typical' not documented → treat missing as not typical and report separately.; Head injury during seizure is common → use NICE head injury criteria as the agreed 'other concern'.; Radiology vetting changes between cycles → keep the same vetting rules.; Fear of litigation → present the zero-miss results from cycle 1 CT findings.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED consultant who leads imaging governance.; Show the number of new findings in cycle 1; usually near zero.; Pair with an ED seizure proforma that records 'typical: yes/no'.; Keep a named radiology link for the second cycle.

**NNA-130. Do adults with epilepsy have a discussion of their risk of sudden unexpected death in epilepsy (SUDEP) documented in a clinic letter within 12 months?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 10.1.4 (2022, updated 2026): "Discuss with people with epilepsy, and their families and carers if appropriate, their individual risk of epilepsy-related death, including SUDEP, from the time of diagnosis onwards." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/10-Reducing-the-risk-of-epilepsy-related-death-including-sudden-unexpected-death-in-epilepsy
- Pass: A clinic letter within the last 12 months records a discussion of epilepsy-related death or SUDEP risk with the person (or carer), including at least one risk-reduction point.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive adults seen in epilepsy clinics over the last 2 months (look back 12 months in their letters).
- Change: Add a 'SUDEP/epilepsy-related death risk discussed: yes/no, advice given' line to the epilepsy clinic letter template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-130.csv`
- Pitfalls: Discussion held but not written → the template line fixes documentation, not only practice.; Clinicians worry about distressing patients → share the NICE rationale and patient charity leaflets.; Letters from other hospitals not visible → use only local letters and state this.; Definition drift between cycles → fix the 12-month look-back.
- Pearls: Recruit an epilepsy specialist nurse as co-lead.; Include a SUDEP leaflet link in the template.; Agree the template wording with all consultants before cycle 1.; Report by clinician privately to encourage uptake.

**NNA-131. Do adults admitted with convulsive status epilepticus leave hospital with an agreed individualised emergency management plan?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 7.1.12 (2022, updated 2026): "After an episode of convulsive status epilepticus, agree an emergency management plan with the person if they do not already have one and there is concern that status epilepticus may recur." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/7-Treating-status-epilepticus-repeated-or-cluster-seizures-and-prolonged-seizures
- Pass: Discharge letter or care plan contains an emergency management plan (what to give, dose, who can give it, when to call 999), or a documented existing plan reviewed, or a documented decision that recurrence is not a concern.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: All eligible admissions over the last 12 months (expect 30–40).
- Change: Automatic referral to the epilepsy specialist nurse for every status epilepticus admission, with a standard emergency plan template attached to the discharge letter.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-131.csv`
- Pitfalls: Coding misses status recorded as 'seizure' → also screen admissions with IV lorazepam given.; 'Concern of recurrence' judged differently → agree the definition with neurology before cycle 1.; Epilepsy nurse capacity → estimate yearly numbers from cycle 1.; Plans written but not shared with carers → collect carer training as a separate field.
- Pearls: Recruit the epilepsy nurse team as owners.; Use the pharmacy dispensing log for buccal midazolam to find cases.; Store the plan template on the intranet next to the discharge letter template.; Present to acute medicine, where most patients are discharged from.

**NNA-132. Do adults with Parkinson's disease on a dopamine agonist have a discussion about impulse control disorders documented at their most recent review?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG71 rec 1.4.4 (2017): "Discuss potential impulse control disorders at review appointments, particularly when modifying therapy, and record that the discussion has taken place." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng71/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The most recent Parkinson's clinic letter records that impulse control disorders were asked about or discussed.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients seen over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add an 'impulse control disorders asked (patient and carer): yes/no' line to the Parkinson's clinic letter template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-132.csv`
- Pitfalls: Discussion done but not recorded → the template line fixes this.; Letters written by different clinicians use different headings → agree the template with all clinicians.; Levodopa-only patients also at risk → optional second cohort, but keep cycle 1 and 2 identical.; Patients embarrassed → include carer questioning as a separate field.
- Pearls: Recruit the Parkinson's specialist nurse.; Use the QUIP short questionnaire if clinicians want a tool, but only the discussion is audited.; Link to NICE rec 1.4.3 (information at start of agonist) for a second cycle.; Share a one-line script for asking about gambling, shopping, eating and sex.

**NNA-133. How many inpatients with Parkinson's disease are prescribed haloperidol or prochlorperazine?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG71 rec 1.5.20 (2017): "Recognise that other antipsychotic medicines (such as phenothiazines and butyrophenones) can worsen the motor features of Parkinson's disease." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng71/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No haloperidol, prochlorperazine or other phenothiazine or butyrophenone prescribed (regular or as required) during the admission, unless a Parkinson's specialist decision is documented.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions over the last 2 months (EPMA report of Parkinson's medicines).
- Change: Add an EPMA alert that fires when haloperidol, prochlorperazine or metoclopramide is prescribed to a patient with levodopa or a dopamine agonist on the chart, suggesting safer alternatives.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-133.csv`
- Pitfalls: Prescribing from order sets (for example post-operative nausea) → review antiemetic order sets too.; Metoclopramide not named in the NICE wording → audit it as a separate field.; Alert fatigue → restrict the alert to the patients with Parkinson's drugs on the chart.; Delirium pathways recommend haloperidol → update the delirium guideline at the same time.
- Pearls: Recruit an EPMA pharmacist to build the report and the alert.; Offer domperidone and ondansetron alternatives in the alert text.; Involve the Parkinson's nurse in the governance paper.; Re-run the EPMA report quarterly to keep gains.

**NNA-134. Do older adults diagnosed with delirium in hospital have delirium recorded in their discharge summary to the GP?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG103 rec 1.6.4 (2010, updated 2023): "Ensure that the diagnosis of delirium is documented both in the person's record or notes, and in their primary care health record." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Discharge summary lists delirium (current or resolved) as a diagnosis, with a recommendation for follow-up if unresolved.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'Delirium during admission: yes/no/resolved' field to the electronic discharge summary for patients aged 65+.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-134.csv`
- Pitfalls: Delirium present but never named in notes → include only patients with a documented diagnosis or positive 4AT.; Field ticked 'no' by default → make it an active choice without a default.; GPs may not read the field → ask one local GP to review sample summaries.; Short-stay patients discharged by juniors unaware → include the field in the discharge checklist.
- Pearls: Recruit a frailty consultant and the clinical coding lead.; Show the coding income gain; managers often support it.; Add a single-line leaflet for families about delirium to the discharge pack.; Assign a named ward registrar to check the field weekly for 2 months.

**NNA-135. Do older inpatients given haloperidol for delirium have a baseline ECG before the first dose?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update: haloperidol in elderly patients with delirium (10 December 2021): "before initiating treatment, a baseline electrocardiogram (ECG) and correction of any electrolyte disturbances is recommended; cardiac and electrolyte monitoring should be repeated during treatment" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/haloperidol-haldol-reminder-of-risks-when-used-in-elderly-patients-for-the-acute-treatment-of-delirium
- Pass: 12-lead ECG with QTc recorded before, or within 24 hours before, the first haloperidol dose (emergency use with a documented reason counts as a pass if an ECG follows within 24 hours).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive patients over the last 3 months (EPMA administration report).
- Change: Link the haloperidol prescription in EPMA to a required field 'Baseline ECG QTc (ms) and date', with an override reason for emergencies.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-135.csv`
- Pitfalls: Emergency doses given at night before an ECG is possible → allow a 24-hour catch-up with a documented reason.; Haloperidol used as antiemetic confuses the sample → filter by indication in notes.; ECG done but QTc not read → require the QTc value.; Pushback about delaying treatment → stress non-drug measures come first (NICE CG103 rec 1.7.3).
- Pearls: Recruit an EPMA pharmacist and a frailty consultant.; Also record Parkinson's or Lewy body disease; any use there is a separate safety finding.; Use the ECG system to confirm times, not the notes.; Present the result alongside the NICE delirium pathway to keep it in context.

**NNA-136. Do inpatients with dementia have their anticholinergic burden score recorded during their hospital medication review?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG97 rec 1.6.2 (2018): "Consider minimising the use of medicines associated with increased anticholinergic burden, and if possible look for alternatives: when assessing whether to refer a person with suspected dementia for diagnosis during medication reviews with people living with dementia." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: An anticholinergic burden score (any validated scale, for example ACB) documented during the admission, and, where the score is 3 or more, a documented decision on each contributing drug (stop, reduce, switch or continue with reason).. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add an automatic ACB score to the pharmacy medicines reconciliation template in EPR for patients with a dementia flag, displayed on the ward round summary.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-136.csv`
- Pitfalls: Score calculated but no action → audit the decision step as part of the pass.; Different ACB scales between cycles → fix one scale before cycle 1.; Psychiatric drugs started by others → document 'continue with reason' as a valid decision.; Short stays → limit to stays of 72 hours or more.
- Pearls: Recruit the ward pharmacist as co-lead.; Print the top 10 local anticholinergic drugs with alternatives on the ward round sheet.; Send the score to the GP in the discharge summary so the change lasts.; Keep the report in the monthly pharmacy dashboard.

**NNA-137. When an antipsychotic is started in hospital for a person with dementia, is a discussion of benefits and harms with the person or their family documented before the first regular dose?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG97 rec 1.7.5 (2018): "Before starting antipsychotics, discuss the benefits and harms with the person and their family members or carers (as appropriate). Consider using a decision aid to support this discussion." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Notes record a discussion of benefits and harms with the person, their family, carer or attorney before or within 24 hours of the first regular antipsychotic dose, with the planned review date.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive new starts over the last 6 months (EPMA report).
- Change: Add a short 'Antipsychotic in dementia' checklist to the EPMA prescription (non-drug measures tried, discussion with person or family, review date) that must be completed for a regular prescription.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-137.csv`
- Pitfalls: Night-time PRN doses become regular without discussion → count the first regular dose as the trigger.; Family unreachable → accept a documented attempt with time and number called.; Psychiatry liaison starts drug → the standard still applies; include them in the change.; Checklist ticked without discussion → spot-check 5 notes for a free-text record.
- Pearls: Recruit the dementia lead nurse and liaison psychiatry.; Print the NICE patient decision aid for ward packs.; Agree what counts as 'regular' with pharmacy before cycle 1.; Report antipsychotics continued at discharge as a secondary outcome for GPs.

**NNA-138. Do inpatients who fall have a medical examination documented within 12 hours of the fall?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE QS86 statement 6 (2015, quality standard updated 2025): "People who fall during a hospital stay have a medical examination. [Quality measure a: Proportion of falls in people during a hospital stay where the person has a medical examination completed within 12 hours.]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/qs86/chapter/Quality-statement-6-Medical-examination-after-an-inpatient-fall
- Pass: Documented medical examination (by a doctor or advanced practitioner) within 12 hours of the recorded fall time, covering injury check and a cause assessment.. Target: ≥90% within 12 hours. Sample: 40 consecutive inpatient falls over the last 2 months.
- Change: Introduce a post-fall medical review sticker/electronic form triggered by the incident report, with fields for injury check, cause and CT decision.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-138.csv`
- Pitfalls: Fall time missing on incident report → use the nursing entry time and flag it.; Reviews not documented at night → record night falls separately.; Incident system under-reports falls → cross-check with ward safety huddle records.; Doctors see it as extra work → keep the form short (6 fields).
- Pearls: Recruit the falls prevention nurse and ward sisters.; Use the incident system as the sampling frame so both cycles match.; Report the 30-minute injury subgroup separately (NAIF measure).; Include the form in junior doctor induction and bleep holder handbook.

**NNA-139. Do older inpatients who fall in hospital have a documented review of fall-risk-increasing medicines within 72 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG249 rec 1.3.17 (2025): "As part of a structured medication review, consider making appropriate adjustments to the person's medicines to reduce the risk of falls. Also see the section on medication review in the NICE guideline on medicines optimisation and the section on reviewing medicines in the NICE guideline on medicines adherence." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng249/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Within 72 hours of the fall, the notes record a review of fall-risk-increasing drugs (for example sedatives, antipsychotics, antihypertensives, opioids, anticholinergics) with a decision for each (stop, reduce, continue with reason).. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive falls over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a FRIDs checklist section to the post-fall medical review form, with an automatic referral to the ward pharmacist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-139.csv`
- Pitfalls: 'Medications reviewed' written without detail → require a decision per FRID.; Disagreement on the FRID list → adopt a published list (for example STOPPFall) before cycle 1.; Pharmacist capacity → prioritise patients with 3 or more FRIDs.; Changes not passed to GP → include 'changes in discharge summary' as a field.
- Pearls: Recruit the ward pharmacist as co-lead.; Print the FRID list on the post-fall form.; Combine data collection with the 12-hour medical examination audit.; Report the number of FRIDs stopped; it motivates teams.

**NNA-140. Do older people who have taken a bisphosphonate for 3 years or more have a documented discussion about stopping it?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG56 rec 1.6.16 (2016): "Discuss stopping bisphosphonate after 3 years and include patient choice, fracture risk and life expectancy in the discussion." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng56/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Clinic letter, discharge summary or medication review note records a discussion of continuing or stopping the bisphosphonate that mentions fracture risk and the person's view.. Target: ≥75%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible patients over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a 'bisphosphonate 3 years or more: reviewed, decision' line to the care of older people clinic letter and discharge summary templates, triggered by the medication list.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-140.csv`
- Pitfalls: Start date unknown → use GP record; exclude if still unknown.; High-risk patients (previous vertebral fracture) should continue → a documented 'continue' decision is a pass.; Clinicians think it is the GP's job → present it as a shared review, copied to the GP.; Numbers too low in one clinic → include ward discharges.
- Pearls: Recruit the fracture liaison nurse.; Provide a one-line FRAX link in the template.; Agree with local GPs who owns the decision after the letter.; Add the template line permanently so it survives rotation.

**NNA-141. Do adults recognised as dying in hospital have anticipatory medicines prescribed with an indication and dose range for each drug?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG31 rec 1.6.1 (2015): "Use an individualised approach to prescribing anticipatory medicines for people who are likely to need symptom control in the last days of life. Specify the indications for use and the dosage of any medicines prescribed." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng31/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Within 24 hours of recognition of dying, as-required medicines are prescribed for pain, agitation, nausea and vomiting, breathlessness and respiratory secretions, each with an indication, dose, route and maximum 24-hour dose, adjusted for renal function where relevant.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive deaths over the last 2–3 months (mortuary or bereavement office list).
- Change: Build an end-of-life anticipatory medicines order set in EPMA with pre-filled indications, dose ranges, maximum doses and a renal-impairment version, agreed with the palliative care team.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-141.csv`
- Pitfalls: Recognition of dying time unclear → use the first documented statement that the person is dying.; Order sets used blindly → keep 'individualise and review daily' wording and a palliative contact.; Palliative team concern about standardisation → co-design the order set with them.; Deaths spread over many wards → sample through the bereavement office list, not wards.
- Pearls: Recruit the hospital palliative care nurse specialist and an EPMA pharmacist.; Link the audit to NACEL so the trust already has a governance route.; Include renal dosing; it is the most common error.; Add the order set to the end-of-life care plan document.

**NNA-142. Do adults in the last days of life in hospital have their hydration status assessed and documented every day?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG31 rec 1.4.4 (2015): "Assess, preferably daily, the dying person's hydration status, and review the possible need for starting clinically assisted hydration, respecting the person's wishes and preferences." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng31/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A hydration assessment (for example oral intake, thirst, mouth, signs of dehydration or overload) and a decision about clinically assisted hydration are documented on every full day between recognition of dying and death.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible deaths over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a daily 'Hydration and mouth care' box to the individualised end-of-life care plan, reviewed at the daily ward round.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-142.csv`
- Pitfalls: Nursing and medical notes in different systems → check both.; 'Comfortable' entries counted as assessment → define what counts before cycle 1.; Families distressed by 'withholding fluids' → pair with the discussion field and leaflet.; Deaths with short dying phase → require 48 hours so every day can be judged.
- Pearls: Recruit the palliative care nurse specialist and ward matrons.; Use NACEL case note review forms to align definitions.; Keep the care plan box short (three lines).; Share one anonymised good example at ward huddles.

**NNA-143. Do older inpatients started on a strong opioid have a regular laxative prescribed at the same time?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG140 rec 1.1.18 (2012, updated 2016): "Prescribe laxative treatment (to be taken regularly at an effective dose) for all patients starting strong opioids." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg140/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A regular (not only as-required) laxative prescribed within 24 hours of the first strong opioid prescription, unless contraindicated (for example bowel obstruction, stoma with high output) and documented.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new strong opioid starts over the last 2 months (EPMA report).
- Change: Link a regular laxative prompt to all strong opioid prescriptions in EPMA (opioid order set includes a laxative that must be accepted or declined with a reason).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-143.csv`
- Pitfalls: As-required laxatives counted → count only regular prescriptions.; Weak opioids (codeine) included by mistake → list strong opioids before cycle 1.; Alert fatigue → only trigger the prompt when no regular laxative is on the chart.; Patients refuse laxatives → still a pass if prescribed; record refusals.
- Pearls: Recruit the ward pharmacist; the EPMA report takes minutes.; Suggest a default stimulant laxative in the order set.; Record bowel outcome as a balancing measure.; Share results with surgery too; the same EPMA change helps them.

**NNA-144. When an adult in hospital is recognised as dying, are medicines that no longer give symptom benefit stopped within 24 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG31 rec 1.5.1 (2015): "When it is recognised that a person may be entering the last days of life, review their current medicines and, after discussion and agreement with the dying person and those important to them (as appropriate), stop any previously prescribed medicines that are not providing symptomatic benefit or that may cause harm." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng31/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Within 24 hours of recognition of dying, a documented medicines review, and statins, bisphosphonates, vitamin supplements, oral hypoglycaemics (unless symptomatic), routine VTE prophylaxis and other non-symptomatic medicines are stopped or have a documented reason to continue.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible deaths over the last 2–3 months.
- Change: Add a 'Medicines review at recognition of dying' checklist to the individualised end-of-life care plan, listing common medicines to stop.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-144.csv`
- Pitfalls: Disagreement on what is 'non-symptomatic' → agree a list with palliative care before cycle 1.; Anticoagulants for AF debated → count a documented reason as a pass.; Families want medicines continued → the discussion field captures this.; Recognition of dying reversed → exclude patients who stabilised and were discharged.
- Pearls: Recruit the palliative care consultant and ward pharmacist.; Run the EPMA 'active prescriptions' report at recognition time for quick data.; Count doses given after recognition; it is a striking figure.; Pair with the anticipatory medicines audit to share data collection.

## Psychiatry

**NNA-145. What proportion of adults seen by liaison psychiatry after self-harm are given a copy of their care plan before they leave the emergency department or ward?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG225 Self-harm: assessment, management and preventing recurrence, rec 1.5.16 (2022): "Give the person a copy of their care plan, and share the plan as soon as possible with relevant healthcare professionals and social care practitioners involved in the person's care." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The liaison record states that a written or printed care plan (including crisis contacts) was given to the person before discharge from the emergency department or ward.. Target: ≥90% given a copy or documented as declined. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible assessments over the last 2 months
- Change: Add a 'print patient copy' step and a mandatory 'copy given: yes/no/declined' tick box to the liaison electronic assessment template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-145.csv`
- Pitfalls: Out-of-hours assessors cannot print in the emergency department → allow a copy by text or email, or a handwritten crisis card, to count as a pass.; Notes say 'plan discussed' but not 'copy given' → define the pass wording before cycle 1 and keep it the same in cycle 2.; People who leave soon after assessment skew the rate → record 'left before copy could be given' separately.; Staff see the tick box as more paperwork → show them the NG225 wording and keep the box to one click.
- Pearls: Recruit the liaison team manager and one band 6 nurse as co-leads; they own the template.; Agree with IT a one-page printable plan built from fields already in the assessment.; Keep a stock of printed crisis cards in the emergency department mental health room.; Hand the monthly count to the team manager so it survives the trainee rotation.

**NNA-146. What proportion of liaison psychiatry self-harm assessments give a reason for discharge that does not rest on a 'low/medium/high' risk rating?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG225 Self-harm, rec 1.6.2 (2022): "Do not use risk assessment tools and scales to determine who should and should not be offered treatment or who should be discharged." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The outcome section gives the reason for the disposal in terms of needs, formulation and safety plan, and does not use a risk score or a 'low/medium/high' category as the stated reason.. Target: ≥90% with a needs-based reason and no risk grade used as the reason. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 2 months
- Change: Replace the 'overall risk: low/medium/high' drop-down in the liaison template with a free-text 'risk formulation and safety plan' box and a 'reason for this disposal' box.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-146.csv`
- Pitfalls: Some staff and commissioners still expect a risk grade → agree with the clinical director before cycle 1 that the grade is being removed on NG225 grounds.; Recording a grade is not a fail if it is not the reason → audit the reason, not the presence of the word.; The drop-down is part of a trust-wide form → pilot a liaison-only version first.; Judging 'needs-based' is subjective → two auditors score the first 10 cases and agree the rule.
- Pearls: Bring the trust suicide prevention lead on side; they usually want this change.; Use real anonymised notes to show the difference between a grade and a formulation.; Pair the template change with the safety plan audit so the box is not left empty.; Ask the governance team to keep the monthly score after the audit team moves on.

**NNA-147. What proportion of patients physically restrained on an adult inpatient ward are offered a documented chance to talk about the incident within 72 hours?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG10 Violence and aggression: short-term management in mental health, health and community settings, rec 1.4.58 (2015): "Ensure that the service user involved has the opportunity to discuss the incident in a supportive environment with a member of staff or an advocate or carer. Offer the service user the opportunity to write their perspective of the event in the notes." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes show that, within 72 hours of the restraint, the patient was offered a discussion of the incident with staff, an advocate or a carer (accepted or declined).. Target: ≥80% offered within 72 hours. Sample: 40 consecutive restraint incidents over the last 3 months
