- Change: Add a 'patient debrief offered' field with a 72-hour due time to the restraint incident form, which triggers a task for the named nurse.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-147.csv`
- Pitfalls: Debriefs happen but are not written down → count only what is documented and say so when presenting.; Staff debrief is confused with patient debrief → keep them as separate fields.; Patients who remain very unwell for days → allow 'deferred, reason recorded' but report it separately.; Ward staff feel judged → frame it as a patient-experience measure and share results by ward, not by name.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward manager, the restrictive practice lead and a peer support worker.; Ask advocacy services to accept debrief referrals directly.; Put the debrief prompt on the safety huddle board for patients restrained in the last 72 hours.; The incident form field keeps the change after the audit team rotates.

**NNA-148. Among detained patients who did not understand their rights at the first explanation, what proportion had their rights explained again within 7 days?** (new)
- Standard: Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice (2015), paragraph 4.28: "Those with responsibility for patient care should ensure that patients are reminded from time to time of their rights and the effects of the Act. It may be necessary to give the same information on a number of different occasions or in different formats and to check regularly that the patient has fully understood it. Information given to a patient who is unwell may need to be repeated when their condition has improved." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5a80a774e5274a2e87dbb0f0/MHA_Code_of_Practice.PDF
- Pass: A further section 132 explanation (with understanding recorded) is documented within 7 days of the first explanation that was recorded as not understood, declined or not possible.. Target: ≥90% repeated within 7 days. Sample: 40 eligible detentions from the last 4 months (use the Mental Health Act office list to find them)
- Change: Make the electronic section 132 form create an automatic 'repeat rights due' task at 7 days whenever the outcome is anything other than 'fully understood'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-148.csv`
- Pitfalls: Paper rights forms are filed but not scanned → agree with the Mental Health Act office to use their tracker as the source.; Repeat explanations are done but written only in nursing notes → search nursing notes as well as forms.; Small numbers on one ward → pool all adult acute wards.; Nurses see it as an administrative task → show the Code wording and the Care Quality Commission focus on rights.
- Pearls: The Mental Health Act administrator is the key ally; they already track rights.; Agree what 'understood' means (patient can say it back in their own words) before cycle 1.; Easy-read and translated leaflets should be at the nursing station, not in an office.; Hand the monthly report to the Mental Health Act office manager.

**NNA-149. What proportion of detained patients recorded as lacking capacity to decide about advocacy are referred to an independent mental health advocate within 7 days?** (new)
- Standard: Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice (2015), paragraph 6.16: "If a patient lacks capacity to decide whether or not to obtain help from an IMHA, the hospital manager should ask an IMHA to attend the patient so that the IMHA can explain what they can offer to the patient directly." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5a80a774e5274a2e87dbb0f0/MHA_Code_of_Practice.PDF
- Pass: An IMHA referral (or IMHA visit) is documented within 7 days of the record that the patient lacked capacity to decide about advocacy or did not understand their rights.. Target: ≥90% referred within 7 days. Sample: 30–40 eligible patients from the last 6 months across adult and older adult wards
- Change: Set up an automatic ('opt-out') IMHA referral: the Mental Health Act office emails the advocacy provider for every patient whose rights form records lack of capacity.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-149.csv`
- Pitfalls: Capacity for advocacy is rarely recorded as such → accept 'did not understand rights' as the trigger and define it before cycle 1.; The advocacy provider keeps its own records → ask them for a referral list for the audit period.; Older adult and learning disability wards may have few cases → pool across wards.; Concern that opt-out referral breaches consent → the Code allows it for patients who lack capacity; confirm with the Mental Health Act lead.
- Pearls: Invite the advocacy service manager to the planning meeting; they often want this data.; Use the Mental Health Act office as the single point that sends referrals.; Include older adult wards, where lack of capacity is most common.; Add the referral date to the rights tracker so it is checked every month.

**NNA-150. What proportion of section 17 leave authorisations have a documented copy given to the patient?** (new)
- Standard: Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice (2015), paragraph 27.22: "Copies of the authorisation should be given to the patient and to any carers, professionals and other people in the community who need to know. A copy should also be kept in the patient's notes." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5a80a774e5274a2e87dbb0f0/MHA_Code_of_Practice.PDF
- Pass: The leave form or notes record that a copy was given to the patient (or offered and declined).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive leave authorisations over the last 2 months
- Change: Add a 'copy given to patient: yes/declined' box and a patient signature line to the section 17 form, completed by the nurse who explains the leave.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-150.csv`
- Pitfalls: Copies are given but not recorded → the new box makes it visible; do not count cycle 1 as bad practice without explanation.; Forms are scanned late → check the ward folder as well as the electronic record.; Responsible clinicians change forms often → count each new authorisation as one case.; Staff worry patients will lose the form → offer a copy kept in the patient's room or locker.
- Pearls: Recruit the Mental Health Act administrator and one ward manager.; Agree that 'offered and declined' counts as a pass.; Ask responsible clinicians to hand the form to the named nurse straight after the ward round.; Add the item to the existing Mental Health Act office spot checks.

**NNA-151. What proportion of people brought to the section 136 place of safety are seen by both the doctor and the AMHP within 3 hours of arrival?** (under-audited)
- Standard: Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice (2015), paragraph 16.47: "Assessment by the doctor and AMHP should begin as soon as possible after the arrival of the individual at the place of safety. In cases where there are no clinical grounds to delay assessment, it is good practice for the doctor and AMHP to attend within three hours; this is in accordance with best practice recommendations made by the Royal College of Psychiatrists." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5a80a774e5274a2e87dbb0f0/MHA_Code_of_Practice.PDF
- Pass: Both the doctor and the approved mental health professional (AMHP) attended within 3 hours of arrival, or a clinical reason for delay (for example, intoxication) is recorded.. Target: ≥80% within 3 hours or with a recorded reason. Sample: 40 consecutive s136 detentions (usually 1–2 months in a typical suite)
- Change: Send an automatic page to both the section 12 doctor rota and the AMHP hub when the place of safety records arrival, with a 2-hour escalation to the on-call consultant.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-151.csv`
- Pitfalls: The AMHP service is run by the local authority → involve the AMHP lead from the start; they own half of the target.; Arrival time is recorded differently by police and staff → use the place of safety log time only.; Intoxicated patients inflate breaches → record the clinical reason and report both rates.; Staffing shortages at night → split results by in-hours and out-of-hours to target the fix.
- Pearls: Get the s136 multi-agency meeting to sign off the audit in advance.; Record the reason for each breach using fixed codes.; Keep the escalation rule simple: 2 hours without a doctor or AMHP means a call to the consultant.; Hand the monthly rate to the place of safety manager.

**NNA-152. What proportion of people with schizophrenia who have not responded to two antipsychotics have a documented offer of clozapine?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG178 Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults, rec 1.5.7.2 (2014, updated 2026): "Offer clozapine to people with schizophrenia whose illness has not responded adequately to treatment despite the sequential use of adequate doses of at least 2 different antipsychotic drugs. At least 1 of the drugs should be a non-clozapine second-generation antipsychotic." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes record that clozapine was offered (accepted or declined) or give a documented contraindication.. Target: ≥80% offered or contraindication recorded. Sample: 30–40 eligible people from 2 community teams (find them by searching prescribing history)
- Change: Add a 'treatment resistance check' line to the annual care plan review template: number of adequate trials, and 'clozapine offered: yes/no/declined/contraindicated'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-152.csv`
- Pitfalls: Defining 'adequate trial' is contentious → agree dose and duration with the lead consultant before cycle 1.; Old records are incomplete → accept clinic letters as evidence of past trials.; Consultants may feel criticised → present as a caseload opportunity, not individual failure.; Offers are made verbally and not written → the template line makes it visible.
- Pearls: Recruit the clozapine clinic pharmacist; they can search dispensing records quickly.; Share a patient leaflet on clozapine to support the discussion.; Present anonymised examples of people who waited years for clozapine.; Ask the pharmacy team to run the caseload search yearly after the audit.

**NNA-153. What proportion of families of people in early intervention in psychosis services have a documented offer of family intervention?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG178 Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults, rec 1.4.4.2 (2014, updated 2026): "Offer family intervention to all families of people with psychosis or schizophrenia who live with or are in close contact with the service user (delivered as described in recommendation 1.3.7.2). This can be started either during the acute phase or later, including in inpatient settings." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes record that family intervention was offered to the family (accepted or declined) within 6 months of acceptance to the service.. Target: ≥90% offered. Sample: 40 consecutive people accepted 6–12 months ago
- Change: Add a 'family intervention offered: yes/declined/not applicable' field to the 3-month early intervention care plan review.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-153.csv`
- Pitfalls: Too few trained family therapists → audit the offer, not delivery; report waiting time separately.; Family work is recorded in a separate psychology system → check both systems.; The service user refuses family involvement → record and exclude.; The field is ticked without a real offer → ask for the offer date and who made it.
- Pearls: Recruit the team psychologist and a family work-trained care coordinator.; Agree a standard family intervention leaflet to hand over at the offer.; Link the field to the carer assessment offer so both happen at one visit.; Hand quarterly reporting to the team manager.

**NNA-154. What proportion of adults admitted with psychosis have a documented offer of individual cognitive behavioural therapy for psychosis before discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG178 Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults, rec 1.4.4.1 (2014, updated 2026): "Offer CBT to all people with psychosis or schizophrenia (delivered as described in recommendation 1.3.7.1). This can be started either during the acute phase or later, including in inpatient settings." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes or discharge summary record that cognitive behavioural therapy for psychosis (CBTp) was offered (accepted, declined or referral made), either on the ward or through the community team.. Target: ≥80%. Sample: 40 consecutive discharges over the last 3 months
- Change: Add a 'psychological therapy (CBTp) offered or referred' line to the discharge summary template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-154.csv`
- Pitfalls: No ward psychologist → a referral to the community team counts; agree this before cycle 1.; Short, chaotic admissions → exclude stays under 7 days.; Psychology notes kept separately → include them in the search.; Offers without capacity to deliver → report waiting lists to the psychological therapies lead.
- Pearls: Recruit the ward psychologist as co-lead.; Keep the discharge summary line short with fixed options.; Link with the family intervention audit so both are offered together.; Ask the discharge coordinator to check the line.

**NNA-155. What proportion of 18–25 year olds started on an antidepressant by a mental health team are reviewed within 7 days?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG222 Depression in adults: treatment and management, rec 1.4.11 (2022): "When prescribing antidepressant medication, ensure people have information about: ... when their first review will be; this will usually be within 2 weeks to check their symptoms are improving and for side effects, or 1 week after starting antidepressant medication if a new prescription is for a person aged 18 to 25 years or if there is a particular concern for risk of suicide" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng222/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A documented review (face-to-face, video or telephone) by the prescriber or team within 7 days of the start date.. Target: ≥85%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive new starts over the last 4 months
- Change: Add an age check to the prescribing entry template: if 18–25 and a new antidepressant, book a 7-day contact before the appointment ends.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-155.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few new starts in one team → pool community, crisis and early intervention teams.; Reviews done by care coordinators are not always recorded as medication reviews → accept any contact that asks about side effects and mood.; Start date unclear → use the date of the first prescription.; GP-started antidepressants muddy the sample → include only team-started prescriptions.
- Pearls: Recruit a team pharmacist to pull new antidepressant starts.; Agree that a telephone call counts.; Put the 7-day rule on the crisis team whiteboard.; Hand the monthly check to the team's lead nurse.

**NNA-156. What proportion of young people started on fluoxetine for depression in CAMHS are receiving psychological therapy at the same time?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG134 Depression in children and young people, rec 1.6.14 (2019): "Do not offer antidepressant medication to a child or young person with moderate to severe depression except in combination with a concurrent psychological therapy." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng134/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A psychological therapy session is recorded within 4 weeks before or after the antidepressant start date, or the notes record that therapy was declined.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive new starts over the last 6 months
- Change: Add a required 'current psychological therapy (type, therapist) or declined' field to the CAMHS medication start form.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-156.csv`
- Pitfalls: Long therapy waits → record 'on waiting list' separately and report it.; Therapy delivered by schools or charities is not in the record → ask families at review and record it.; Small numbers → pool teams or extend to 6 months.; Prescribers feel blamed for service gaps → frame it as a capacity measure.
- Pearls: Recruit a CAMHS psychiatrist and the psychological therapies lead.; Agree what counts as therapy before cycle 1 (named, structured therapy).; Present both prescribing and waiting-list data.; Make the field part of the prescribing template so it survives rotation.

**NNA-157. What proportion of young people moving from CAMHS to adult mental health services have a named transition worker recorded?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG43 Transition from children's to adults' services for young people using health or social care services, rec 1.2.5 (2016): "Help the young person to identify a single practitioner – who should act as a 'named worker' – to coordinate their transition care and support. This person could be supported by an administrator." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng43/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The notes name one practitioner as the transition or named worker before the date of transfer.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive transfers over the last 6–12 months
- Change: Add a 'named transition worker' field to the CAMHS transition referral form, which the adult team must accept by name.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-157.csv`
- Pitfalls: Transfers are few → collect over 12 months and keep a running list.; Two services with different records → get access to both before cycle 1.; Arguments over who is the named worker → the protocol should say who by default.; Young people disengage before transfer → record them separately.
- Pearls: Recruit one CAMHS and one adult team manager.; Invite a young person with lived experience to comment on the form.; Start with a list of all 17.5-year-olds on the CAMHS caseload.; Hand the list to the transition lead to keep it current.

**NNA-158. What proportion of adults with a learning disability prescribed an antipsychotic for behaviour that challenges have a current behaviour support plan or psychological intervention recorded?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG11 Challenging behaviour and learning disabilities, rec 1.8.2 (2015): "Only offer antipsychotic medication in combination with psychological or other interventions." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng11/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A behaviour support plan (or other named psychological intervention) dated within the last 12 months is in the record.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 eligible adults (full caseload search if smaller)
- Change: Add 'current behaviour support plan (date and author)' as a required field in the STOMP medication review template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-158.csv`
- Pitfalls: Behaviour plans are held by care providers → ask providers for a copy and record where it is kept.; Out-of-date plans → define 'current' as dated in the last 12 months.; Few psychologists → accept plans from trained care-provider staff.; Overlap with other STOMP audits → state clearly that this measures the plan only.
- Pearls: Recruit the team psychologist or positive behaviour support lead.; Use the STOMP register as the sampling frame.; Send a standard letter to providers asking for the current plan.; Keep the field in the review template so it is checked at every review.

**NNA-159. What proportion of patients under 55 newly started on valproate by mental health services have two specialists' agreement documented?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, February 2025: Valproate – review by two specialists is required for initiating valproate: "valproate must not be started in new patients (male or female) younger than 55 years unless two specialists independently consider and document that there is no other effective or tolerated treatment, or there are compelling reasons that the reproductive risks do not apply." https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-belvo-convulex-depakote-dyzantil-epilim-epilim-chrono-or-chronosphere-episenta-epival-and-syonellv-review-by-two-specialists-is-required-for-initiating-valproate-but-not-for-male-patients-already-taking-valproate
- Pass: Two separate specialist entries (or a signed initiation form) are on record before or at the start of valproate, each stating no other effective or tolerated treatment or a compelling reason.. Target: 100%. Sample: All new starts in the last 12 months (usually 20–40 in a mid-sized trust)
- Change: Make pharmacy unable to supply a first valproate prescription for anyone under 55 until the trust two-specialist form is attached in the electronic record.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-159.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few new starts → include the whole trust and 12 months.; Unclear who counts as a specialist → use the MHRA list and agree it with pharmacy.; Inpatient starts at night → allow a second opinion within a set time and audit that time.; Pharmacy block delays treatment → agree an urgent route through the on-call consultant.
- Pearls: The chief pharmacist is the key ally; they can pull every new start.; Keep a single trust form for the two opinions.; Report men and women separately.; Keep the pharmacy check after the audit ends.

**NNA-160. What proportion of psychiatric inpatients co-prescribed a benzodiazepine and an opioid have a documented reason and monitoring plan?** (new)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, March 2020: Benzodiazepines and opioids – reminder of risk of potentially fatal respiratory depression: "only prescribe benzodiazepines (or benzodiazepine-like drugs) and opioids together if there is no alternative; if a decision is made to co-prescribe, use the lowest doses possible for the shortest duration of time and carefully monitor patients for signs of respiratory depression" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/benzodiazepines-and-opioids-reminder-of-risk-of-potentially-fatal-respiratory-depression
- Pass: The notes record why co-prescription is needed and a monitoring plan (for example, observations or sedation checks), within 24 hours of the second drug being charted.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive co-prescribed patients over the last 3–6 months
- Change: Add an electronic prescribing alert when a benzodiazepine and an opioid are charted together, requiring a reason and a monitoring plan before the second drug is saved.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-160.csv`
- Pitfalls: PRN opioids for pain are often forgotten → search the full chart, not just regular items.; Alert fatigue → limit the alert to the benzodiazepine–opioid pair.; Paper charts on some wards → use a pharmacist ward round to find cases.; Addictions teams feel second-guessed → agree the monitoring template with them.
- Pearls: Recruit a ward pharmacist; they see every chart.; Include Z-drugs and gabapentinoids as a secondary field if the team wants.; Use a simple sedation scale on the observation chart.; Report methadone cases separately because of the 2-week delay in effect.

**NNA-161. What proportion of olanzapine long-acting injections are followed by a documented 3-hour observation period?** (new)
- Standard: ZypAdhera (olanzapine pamoate) Summary of Product Characteristics, section 4.2 (EMA product information): "After each injection, patients should be observed in a health care facility by appropriately qualified personnel for at least 3 hours for signs and symptoms consistent with olanzapine overdose. Immediately prior to leaving the health care facility, it should be confirmed that the patient is alert, oriented, and absent of any signs and symptoms of overdose." https://www.ema.europa.eu/en/documents/product-information/zypadhera-epar-product-information_en.pdf
- Pass: The injection record shows observation for at least 3 hours after the injection and a documented check that the patient was alert and oriented before leaving.. Target: 100%. Sample: 40 consecutive injections (several per patient allowed) over the last 3 months
- Change: Introduce a one-page olanzapine embonate observation sheet with timed checks at 0, 1, 2 and 3 hours and a discharge sign-off, filed with each injection.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-161.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few patients on this drug → include every injection over 3–6 months.; Patients leave early → record it as a fail and note the reason.; Home visits cannot meet the rule → flag any home injection as a safety incident.; Timings recorded loosely → the timed sheet fixes this.
- Pearls: Recruit the depot clinic lead nurse.; Book a 3-hour clinic slot for each injection.; Give patients a leaflet about the observation and not driving that day.; Keep the sheet in the depot procedure.

**NNA-162. What proportion of patients on community methadone or buprenorphine admitted to a psychiatric ward have their dose and last dispensing confirmed before the first inpatient dose?** (new)
- Standard: Drug misuse and dependence: UK guidelines on clinical management (2017), section 7.5.4.2: "The hospital doctor should ascertain, by independent means, the likely dose of methadone or buprenorphine to which the patient is tolerant, by confirming: the daily dose prescribed; when the last dose was dispensed; whether recently the medication has been collected regularly; the time the last dose was observed to be taken by supervised consumption." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/5a821e3340f0b62305b92945/clinical_guidelines_2017.pdf
- Pass: Before the first inpatient dose, the notes record the prescribed dose and the date of the last dispensed dose confirmed with the community pharmacy or prescriber.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive admissions over the last 6 months
- Change: Add an opioid substitution section to the admission clerking template with fields for pharmacy name, confirmed dose, last dispensed date and time, and prescription cancelled.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-162.csv`
- Pitfalls: Out-of-hours admissions when pharmacies are closed → allow a 'withhold until confirmed' plan and record it.; Patient report alone used → count it as a fail.; Few cases → pool all adult wards over 6 months.; Double supply risk if community prescription not cancelled → include this field.
- Pearls: Recruit the dual diagnosis lead and a ward pharmacist.; Keep a list of local pharmacy opening hours and the out-of-hours addictions contact.; Build the section into the clerking template, not a separate form.; Ask the pharmacist to check the section on the next working day.

**NNA-163. What proportion of pregnant women with a severe mental illness under mental health services have a written perinatal care plan shared with maternity and the GP?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health, rec 1.6.6 (2014, updated 2020): "Professionals in secondary mental health services, including specialist perinatal mental health services, should develop a written care plan in collaboration with a woman who has or has had a severe mental illness. ... The care plan should be recorded in all versions of the woman's notes (her own records and maternity, primary care and mental health notes) and a copy given to the woman and all involved professionals." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A written care plan covering pregnancy, birth and the postnatal period is on record by 32 weeks and has been sent to both maternity and the GP.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive women over the last 6–12 months
- Change: Use a single perinatal care plan template with a built-in distribution list (woman, maternity, GP, health visitor) sent from the electronic record.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-163.csv`
- Pitfalls: Maternity records are in a different system → ask the maternity mental health midwife to check receipt.; Plans made late → track 32 weeks as the deadline.; Small numbers → allow 6–12 months.; Different templates across teams → agree one before cycle 1.
- Pearls: Recruit the specialist mental health midwife.; Use a caseload list with due dates.; Ask maternity to flag the plan on the booking record.; Make the distribution list automatic.

**NNA-164. What proportion of adults with anorexia nervosa who have been underweight for 2 years have had a bone density scan considered?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG69 Eating disorders: recognition and treatment, rec 1.10.15 (2017, updated 2020): "Consider a bone mineral density scan: after 1 year of underweight in children and young people, or earlier if they have bone pain or recurrent fractures; after 2 years of underweight in adults, or earlier if they have bone pain or recurrent fractures." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng69/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A bone density (DXA) scan has been done or requested, or the notes record a decision not to scan with a reason.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 eligible patients from the caseload
- Change: Add a 'bone health: DXA due/done/decided against' line to the eating disorder annual review template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-164.csv`
- Pitfalls: Duration of underweight hard to find → accept the date of first recorded low BMI.; DXA requested by GP or endocrinology → search radiology and GP letters.; 'Consider' is soft wording → the pass counts a reasoned decision either way.; Few patients in adult services → include CAMHS with the 1-year rule.
- Pearls: Recruit the service's physical health lead or dietitian.; Agree the underweight definition before cycle 1.; Explain results to patients as NG69 advises.; Keep the template line to maintain the change.

**NNA-165. What proportion of people newly diagnosed with dementia by the memory service have a named care coordinator recorded in the diagnosis letter?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG97 Dementia: assessment, management and support, rec 1.3.1 (2018): "Provide people living with dementia with a single named health or social care professional who is responsible for coordinating their care." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The diagnosis letter (to the person and GP) names one professional, with contact details, who coordinates care.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new diagnoses over the last 3 months
- Change: Add a required 'your named care coordinator' box with name and phone number to the diagnosis letter template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-165.csv`
- Pitfalls: Services discharge to GP after diagnosis → the GP or dementia adviser can be the named person; agree this before cycle 1.; Letters have a team phone number only → a team number without a name is a fail.; Dementia adviser services run by charities → include them as coordinators.; Letter templates owned by admin staff → involve the admin lead.
- Pearls: Recruit the memory service lead nurse.; Check both the patient and GP letters.; Use the dementia adviser service as the default coordinator where available.; Make the box mandatory in the template.

**NNA-166. What proportion of people with bipolar disorder discharged from secondary care to their GP have a crisis plan with early warning signs in the discharge letter?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG185 Bipolar disorder: assessment and management, rec 1.9.4 (2014, updated 2025): "When making transfer arrangements for a return to primary care, agree a care plan with the person, which includes: ... a crisis plan indicating early warning symptoms and triggers of both mania and depression relapse and preferred response during relapse, including liaison and referral pathways ... Give the person and their GP a copy of the plan, and encourage the person to share it with their carers." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg185/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: The discharge letter or attached plan lists early warning signs and triggers for both mania and depression, and says how to re-refer.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30–40 consecutive discharges over the last 6 months
- Change: Add a bipolar crisis plan section (mania signs, depression signs, triggers, how to re-refer) to the community discharge letter template.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-166.csv`
- Pitfalls: Plans exist in care plans but not in letters → the pass is in the letter sent to the GP.; Warning signs written for one pole only → require both.; Few discharges → pool teams over 6 months.; Template changes need IT time → start with a Word template if needed.
- Pearls: Recruit a consultant and a care coordinator from one team.; Ask a GP for feedback on the new section.; Reuse the relapse signature from the care plan.; Keep the section in the letter template.

**NNA-167. What proportion of people in out-of-area rehabilitation placements have had placement reviews at 3 months and then every 6 months?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG181 Rehabilitation for adults with complex psychosis, rec 1.5.13 (2020): "A designated care manager (or 'out-of-area placement review officer') based within the community mental health rehabilitation team, should review the person's placement after the first 3 months and then every 6 months, to ensure it still meets their needs." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng181/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Documented placement reviews at 3 months (±4 weeks) after placement and then at intervals of no more than 6 months.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All current out-of-area placements (usually 20–40)
- Change: Set up a shared out-of-area placement tracker with review due dates that flags overdue reviews to the rehabilitation team manager each month.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-167.csv`
- Pitfalls: Placements managed by commissioners → agree data sharing before cycle 1.; Reviews done by phone and not written → ask for review reports.; Small numbers → include all current placements.; No designated reviewer → name one as part of the change.
- Pearls: Recruit the rehabilitation consultant and the placement commissioner.; Use the tracker to show time spent out of area.; Share results with families.; Keep the tracker with the team manager.

## Paediatrics and neonatology

**NNA-168. What proportion of infants younger than 3 months with a temperature of 38°C or higher seen by the paediatric team have all four NICE first-line investigations (full blood count, blood culture, CRP and urine test)?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG143 rec 1.5.3 (2019, updated 2021): "Perform the following investigations in infants younger than 3 months with fever: full blood count blood culture C-reactive protein urine testing for urinary tract infection (see the sections on urine collection, preservation and testing in the NICE guideline on urinary tract infection in under 16s) chest X-ray only if respiratory signs are present stool culture, if diarrhoea is present. [2013]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng143/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: FBC, blood culture, CRP and a urine sample sent for testing, all documented within the index attendance (before discharge or within 4 hours of paediatric review).. Target: ≥95% have all four investigations. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible infants over the last 6 months (use ED/PAU coding for fever plus age filter).
- Change: Add a 'febrile infant under 3 months' order set to the EPR (FBC, CRP, blood culture, urine MC&S in one click) and a matching sticker for the PAU clerking proforma.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-168.csv`
- Pitfalls: Urine not obtained before antibiotics in sick infants → record 'attempted, not obtained' separately so the team is not penalised for correct treatment-first care.; Parent-reported fever without a measured temperature → decide before cycle 1 whether to include (NG143 treats parental report as valid); keep the same rule in cycle 2.; Tests sent but sample rejected or clotted → count as sent, and report rejection rate as a secondary finding.; Order set bypassed by locum staff → build it into the triage prompt, not just the paediatric menu.
- Pearls: Recruit an ED paediatric nurse lead; most omissions happen before the paediatrician arrives.; Agree with the lab that urine from under-3-month infants is always cultured, not just dipped.; Run a weekly list from ED triage of age <90 days to capture cases as they happen.; Hand the order set and audit spreadsheet to the next rotation's audit lead at changeover.

**NNA-169. What proportion of children under 5 with fever and at least one amber feature who are discharged without a diagnosis receive a documented safety net?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG143 rec 1.5.25 (2019, updated 2021): "If it is decided that a child does not need to be admitted to hospital, but no diagnosis has been reached, provide a safety net for parents and carers if any 'red' or 'amber' features are present. The safety net should be 1 or more of the following: providing the parent or carer with verbal and/or written information on warning symptoms and how further healthcare can be accessed (see the recommendation on advising parents or carers in the section on advice for home care) arranging further follow-up at a specified time and place liaising with other healthcare professionals, including out-of-hours providers, to ensure direct access for the child if further assessment is required. [2007]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng143/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Notes record at least one of: written fever leaflet given (named), verbal warning symptoms listed, a specified review time and place, or open access arranged.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible discharges over the last 3 months.
- Change: Add a mandatory 'safety net given' field with the four NICE options to the ED/PAU discharge screen, linked to printing the Trust fever leaflet.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-169.csv`
- Pitfalls: Amber features not recorded at all → report traffic-light completion as a secondary measure; do not guess features.; Verbal advice given but not written down → the fix makes the record quick; accept a tick plus leaflet name as evidence.; Staff see a mandatory field as box-ticking → pair it with the printed leaflet so the tick produces something for the family.; Seasonal case mix changes between cycles → compare like-for-like months where possible.
- Pearls: Recruit the ED paediatric consultant and the PAU nurse lead before cycle 1.; Use the Trust or regional fever leaflet already approved; do not write a new one.; Check re-attendance within 7 days as a balancing measure.; Include translated leaflet versions to cover families with limited English.

**NNA-170. What proportion of infants aged 6 weeks and over with bronchiolitis and no underlying condition are started on supplemental oxygen only when saturations are persistently below 90%?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG9 rec 1.4.4 (2015, updated 2021): "Give oxygen supplementation to babies and children with bronchiolitis if their oxygen saturation is: persistently less than 90%, for children aged 6 weeks and over persistently less than 92%, for babies under 6 weeks or children of any age with underlying health conditions. [2021]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng9/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Oxygen started only after documented saturations persistently <90% in air (for this group); or no oxygen given where saturations stayed ≥90%.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible admissions during the current bronchiolitis season.
- Change: Print the NG9 thresholds (90% / 92%) in the oxygen section of the paediatric drug chart or EPR oxygen order, with target range pre-set to 90–94% for this group.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-170.csv`
- Pitfalls: Saturations recorded only after oxygen started → use the triage and first ward readings; mark 'unknown' rather than guess.; Night-time dips and probe artefact → define 'persistent' before cycle 1 (for example 2 readings 15 minutes apart).; Nurses worry about lower targets → share the NICE 2021 rationale and the trial data on readmission.; Small numbers outside winter → collect over the season peak.
- Pearls: Recruit the ward sister and a respiratory nurse; nurses start and wean oxygen.; Record length of stay and readmission within 7 days as balancing measures.; Agree the 'underlying condition' exclusion list at the start.; Keep the chart change in the winter surge plan so it survives rotation.

**NNA-171. What proportion of infants admitted with bronchiolitis receive none of the treatments NICE advises against (antibiotics, salbutamol, nebulised adrenaline, hypertonic saline, ipratropium, montelukast or steroids)?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG9 rec 1.4.3 (2015, updated 2021): "Do not use any of the following to treat bronchiolitis in babies or children: antibiotics hypertonic saline adrenaline (nebulised) salbutamol montelukast ipratropium bromide systemic or inhaled corticosteroids a combination of systemic corticosteroids and nebulised adrenaline. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng9/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: No listed drug given for bronchiolitis during the admission; antibiotics given for a documented separate indication (for example proven UTI, pneumonia with focal signs) count as a pass.. Target: ≥90% receive no listed drug. Sample: 40 consecutive admissions this bronchiolitis season.
- Change: Add a bronchiolitis admission order set in EPMA that contains only supportive care, with a pop-up alert if salbutamol, adrenaline nebs or steroids are prescribed for a patient with bronchiolitis coded.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-171.csv`
- Pitfalls: Trial of salbutamol in older infants with wheeze → agree before cycle 1 whether a documented single trial in over-12-month infants counts as a fail; keep the rule fixed.; Diagnosis coded as bronchiolitis but clinically viral wheeze → exclude only if the notes state the alternative diagnosis.; ED doses before admission → include ED drug records, as that is where most doses are given.; Pop-up fatigue → alert only on the listed drugs, not all nebulisers.
- Pearls: Include the ED team; most salbutamol is given before the ward.; Share each case of a listed drug with the prescriber anonymously in feedback.; Pair with a parent leaflet explaining why inhalers are not used.; Report results with the winter pressures data so managers see the length-of-stay link.

**NNA-172. What proportion of children and young people treated for diabetic ketoacidosis have their intravenous insulin infusion started 1 to 2 hours after intravenous fluids began?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG18 rec 1.4.36 and 1.4.37 (2015, updated 2023): "Start an intravenous insulin infusion 1 to 2 hours after beginning intravenous fluid therapy in children and young people with DKA. If a child or young person with DKA is using an insulin pump, disconnect the pump when starting intravenous insulin therapy. [2015] ... When treating DKA with intravenous insulin in children and young people, use a soluble insulin infusion at a dosage between 0.05 units/kg/hour and 0.1 units/kg/hour. Do not give bolus doses of intravenous insulin. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng18/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Insulin infusion start time is 60–120 minutes after the first IV fluid start time, at 0.05–0.1 units/kg/hour, with no IV insulin bolus.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible episodes over the last 24 months (typically 20–40 in a district general hospital).
- Change: Build the BSPED DKA calculator output into a pre-printed or EPR DKA pathway with a timed box: 'IV fluids started at __ : __ ; insulin not before __ : __ (60 min) and by __ : __ (120 min)'.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-172.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers → use 24 months retrospectively and report counts, not only percentages.; Fluid start time missing (ED bolus not timed) → take the first recorded IV fluid time from ED notes; mark 'unknown' if absent.; ED and ward teams blame each other → include both in the feedback and the change.; Pump users not disconnected → record as a secondary item, not a pass criterion.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric diabetes nurse specialist as co-lead; they see every DKA case.; Use the BSPED calculator print-out as the data sheet; it already has times.; Case-review any episode with cerebral oedema alongside the audit.; Leave a simple case log so the next trainee can re-audit without searching coding.

**NNA-173. What proportion of children and young people recovering from diabetic ketoacidosis receive their first subcutaneous insulin dose at least 30 minutes before the intravenous insulin is stopped?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG18 rec 1.4.44 and 1.4.45 (2015, updated 2023): "Start subcutaneous insulin in a child or young person with DKA at least 30 minutes before stopping intravenous insulin. [2015] ... For a child or young person with DKA who is using an insulin pump, restart the pump at least 60 minutes before stopping intravenous insulin. Change the insulin cartridge and infusion set and insert the cannula into a new subcutaneous site. [2015]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng18/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Subcutaneous injection given ≥30 minutes (or pump restarted ≥60 minutes) before the IV insulin stop time.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: All eligible episodes over the last 24 months.
- Change: Add a 'transition to subcutaneous insulin' step to the DKA pathway with fields for SC dose time and a 'stop IV insulin at (SC time + 30 min)' box signed by the nurse.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-173.csv`
- Pitfalls: Stop time of IV insulin not recorded → use the infusion chart end time; mark unknown if missing.; Transition timed with a meal and done out of hours by agency staff → include the step in the nursing section, not only medical notes.; Pump users managed by family → record the time the family restarted the pump.; Small numbers → report counts and each failure as a case story.
- Pearls: The diabetes nurse specialist is the natural co-lead.; Link to the fluid–insulin timing audit so one data pass covers both.; Ask the ward pharmacist to check overlap on the EPMA at the ward round.; Store the case list in the diabetes team folder for the next cycle.

**NNA-174. What proportion of babies started on antibiotics for suspected neonatal infection receive the first dose within 1 hour of the decision to treat?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG195 rec 1.15.2 (2021, updated 2026): "If a baby needs antibiotic treatment, give this as soon as possible and always within 1 hour of the decision to treat. [2021]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng195/chapter/Principles-around-use-of-antibiotics-for-early-onset-or-late-onset-neonatal-infection
- Pass: Documented administration time of the first antibiotic dose is ≤60 minutes after the documented decision (or prescription) time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive courses over the last 2 months.
- Change: Introduce a neonatal 'sepsis grab bag' (cannula, culture bottle, pre-calculated benzylpenicillin and gentamicin dose card) and a 'decision time' field on the neonatal antibiotic prescription.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-174.csv`
- Pitfalls: Decision time not recorded → agree to use prescription time; note it underestimates delay.; Postnatal ward babies wait for a neonatal doctor to cannulate → capture cause of delay to target the right fix.; Babies on the NNU skew results → report postnatal ward and NNU separately.; Grab bags not restocked → assign a named band 5 nurse per shift.
- Pearls: Co-lead with a neonatal nurse or ANNP; they give most first doses.; Run a 'mock sepsis' drill on the postnatal ward in week 4.; Display monthly median time on the NNU safety board.; Agree grab-bag restocking with pharmacy before launch.

**NNA-175. What proportion of babies started on antibiotics for risk factors or possible early-onset infection have antibiotics stopped at 36 hours when the NICE stopping criteria are met?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG195 rec 1.21.3 (2012, amended 2026): "For babies who are being treated with intravenous antibiotics because of risk factors for early-onset infection or clinical indicators of possible infection, stop antibiotics at 36 hours if: the blood culture is negative and the initial clinical suspicion of infection was not strong and the baby's clinical condition is reassuring, with no clinical indicators of possible infection (see box 2 for clinical indicators of possible early-onset infection) and the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration are reassuring. [2012, amended 2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng195/chapter/Antibiotics-for-early-onset-neonatal-infection
- Pass: In babies meeting all four stopping criteria, the last antibiotic dose is given within 36 hours (allow up to 40 hours) of the first dose.. Target: ≥90% stopped by 36 hours when criteria met. Sample: 50 consecutive courses over the last 3 months (expect 30–40 to meet stopping criteria).
- Change: Add an automatic 36-hour review prompt to the neonatal antibiotic prescription (EPMA stop date set at 36 hours for early-onset regimens, needing a documented reason to extend).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-175.csv`
- Pitfalls: Culture result not visible at 36 hours → measure lab reporting time too; NICE 1.16.1 supports a 36-hour culture result system.; 'Strong suspicion' is subjective → agree a local definition (for example red flag or two or more non-red-flag indicators) before cycle 1.; CRP repeat taken late → record CRP timing as a secondary item.; Seniors override the stop date → reasons are captured; review them at the stewardship meeting.
- Pearls: Recruit the neonatal pharmacist and a microbiologist.; Use the Kaiser or local EOS calculator result as a secondary descriptor, not a pass criterion.; Report bed-days saved; it wins management support.; Keep the stop-date default in the EPMA build, so it survives rotation.

**NNA-176. What proportion of eligible babies born at 35 weeks or later on antibiotics beyond 36 hours are switched to oral amoxicillin when the 2026 NICE criteria are met?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG195 rec 1.22.1 and 1.22.2 (2026): "For babies born from 35+0 weeks' gestation with negative blood culture who need antibiotics for more than 36 hours because the initial clinical suspicion of infection was strong, consider switching treatment from intravenous to oral antibiotics at 36 hours or thereafter to complete the course (up to 7 days of antibiotics in total) if: the baby's clinical condition is reassuring, with no current clinical indicators of ongoing infection (see box 2 for clinical indicators of possible early-onset neonatal infection) and the levels and trends of C-reactive protein concentration are reassuring and the baby is tolerating oral feeds. [2026] ... Use amoxicillin as the oral antibiotic, unless local bacterial resistance data indicates the need for a different antibiotic. [2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng195/chapter/Antibiotics-for-early-onset-neonatal-infection
- Pass: Eligible baby either switched to oral amoxicillin within 24 hours of meeting all criteria, or has a documented senior reason for staying on IV.. Target: ≥80% switched or reason documented. Sample: 30 consecutive eligible babies over the last 6 months (screen all courses >36 hours).
- Change: Add an 'IV-to-oral switch eligible? yes/no/reason' field to the daily neonatal ward-round template and an amoxicillin oral order to the neonatal EPMA antibiotic order set.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-176.csv`
- Pitfalls: Clinicians unaware of the 2026 change → brief consultants before cycle 1; this is a new recommendation.; Amoxicillin dose for neonates under BNFC review → agree a local dose with pharmacy and microbiology first.; Few eligible babies in small units → combine with a neighbouring unit or extend the window.; Home discharge concerns → start with in-hospital oral switch only; add home care later.
- Pearls: Co-lead with the neonatal pharmacist.; Record days of IV cannula use saved as a family-centred outcome.; Ask the network to share the template so results can be compared.; Put the eligibility field on the ward-round template so it survives staff change.

**NNA-177. What proportion of babies having a lumbar puncture for suspected infection have a blood glucose measured immediately before the procedure?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG195 rec 1.14.4 (2024): "Measure blood glucose in babies immediately before lumbar puncture, so that the cerebrospinal fluid to blood glucose ratio can be calculated. [2024]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng195/chapter/Investigations-before-starting-antibiotics-for-early-onset-or-late-onset-neonatal-infection
- Pass: Blood glucose (point-of-care or lab) recorded within 30 minutes before the LP time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive LPs over the last 6 months (use CSF lab requests to find cases).
- Change: Add 'blood glucose immediately before LP: __ mmol/L at __:__' to the LP procedure sticker or EPR procedure note, and add a paired-glucose item to the LP trolley checklist.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-177.csv`
- Pitfalls: Glucose from a gas taken 2 hours earlier → define 'immediately before' as ≤30 minutes before the LP and keep it fixed.; LP time not recorded → use CSF sample receipt minus transport time only if agreed; otherwise mark unknown.; Traumatic or failed LPs → include; the glucose is still needed.; Low numbers in small units → extend the window to 12 months.
- Pearls: Stick the sticker on the LP pack itself.; Ask biochemistry to flag CSF glucose results without a paired blood glucose.; Include ED and children's ward LPs, not just the NNU.; Record the secondary 4-hour CSF result time to engage the lab.

**NNA-178. What proportion of babies with jaundice noticed in the first 24 hours of life have a serum bilirubin measured within 2 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE CG98 rec 1.2.10 and 1.2.12 (2010, updated 2023): "In all babies with suspected or obvious jaundice in the first 24 hours of life, measure and record the serum bilirubin level urgently (within 2 hours). [2010] ... Arrange a referral to ensure that an urgent medical review is conducted (as soon as possible and within 6 hours) for babies with suspected or obvious jaundice in the first 24 hours of life to exclude pathological causes of jaundice. [2010]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg98/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Serum bilirubin sample time ≤2 hours after the first documented observation of jaundice.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive babies over the last 6 months (search bilirubin requests at age <24 hours plus midwifery notes).
- Change: Add an 'early jaundice (<24 h)' escalation box to the postnatal NEWTT chart that triggers a same-hour bilirubin request and neonatal review call, with the time recorded.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-178.csv`
- Pitfalls: Time jaundice first noticed poorly recorded → use the first midwifery entry mentioning jaundice; record 'unknown' otherwise.; Transcutaneous reading used first → CG98 requires serum under 24 hours; count TcB alone as a fail and report separately.; Night-time phlebotomy delay → capture who took the sample.; Few cases → extend to 12 months if needed.
- Pearls: Co-lead with a postnatal ward midwife.; Add a laminated flowchart at the TcB meter storage point.; Share each missed case at the maternity safety huddle.; Ask the lab to prioritise bilirubin on babies under 24 hours.

**NNA-179. What proportion of babies seen for prolonged jaundice have the full NICE screen, including a conjugated bilirubin?** (new)
- Standard: NICE CG98 rec 1.7.1 (2010, amended 2023): "In babies with a gestational age of 37 weeks or more with jaundice lasting more than 14 days, and in babies with a gestational age of less than 37 weeks and jaundice lasting more than 21 days: look for pale chalky stools and/or dark urine that stains the nappy measure the conjugated bilirubin carry out a full blood count carry out a blood group determination (mother and baby) and DAT (Coombs' test). Interpret the result taking account of the strength of reaction, and whether mother received prophylactic anti‑D immunoglobulin during pregnancy consider a urine culture if there is clinical suspicion of a urinary tract infection ensure that routine metabolic screening (including screening for congenital hypothyroidism) has been performed. [2010, amended 2023]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg98/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Stool and urine colour documented, conjugated bilirubin, FBC, blood group and DAT done, and newborn blood spot result confirmed, at the first prolonged jaundice visit.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive babies over the last 3 months.
- Change: Replace free-text clinic notes with a one-page prolonged jaundice proforma including a stool colour chart and a pre-ticked pathology order set (split bilirubin, FBC, group, DAT).
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-179.csv`
- Pitfalls: Blood group of mother not requested because 'already known' → count as done only if the result is in the record.; Blood spot result not visible to hospital staff → agree access to the child health record before cycle 1.; Babies seen by midwives in the community → include only hospital clinic attendances in both cycles.; DAT not needed in some units' local policy → keep to NICE and note any agreed local variation.
- Pearls: Include a stool colour card; it doubles as parent education.; Recruit the clinic nurse or phlebotomist who runs the session.; Track time to specialist referral for any conjugated bilirubin >25 µmol/L.; Keep the proforma in the clinic template library, not a personal folder.

**NNA-180. What proportion of babies born at or before 30 weeks' gestation start caffeine citrate before 3 days of age?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG124 rec 1.3.5 (2019): "Use caffeine citrate routinely in preterm babies born at or before 30 weeks, starting it as early as possible and ideally before 3 days of age." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng124/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: First caffeine citrate dose given before 72 hours of age.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible babies over the last 12 months (use BadgerNet gestation filter).
- Change: Add caffeine citrate loading and maintenance doses to the ≤30-week admission order set in EPMA, so it is prescribed at admission unless actively removed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-180.csv`
- Pitfalls: Few eligible babies in a local neonatal unit → use 12 months and consider joining with a network NICU.; Babies transferred in after 72 hours → exclude; record transfers separately.; Ventilated babies with delayed start by choice → record documented clinical reason; decide before cycle 1 whether a documented reason counts as a pass.; Order set not updated in the EPMA build → involve the pharmacy EPMA lead early.
- Pearls: The neonatal pharmacist can build the order set in a week.; Look at caffeine stopping at 33–35 weeks (NICE 1.3.6) as a second measure.; Present hours-to-caffeine as a run chart.; Use BadgerNet reports so the next trainee can repeat it in an hour.

**NNA-181. What proportion of preterm babies on supplemental oxygen have a documented target saturation range of 91–95%?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG124 rec 1.4.2 (2019): "After initial stabilisation, aim for an oxygen saturation of 91% to 95% in preterm babies." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng124/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: A written target SpO2 range of 91–95% (on oxygen prescription, observation chart or monitor alarm record) on the audit day.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: Point-prevalence on 10 separate days over 2 weeks (aim for 40 baby-days).
- Change: Pre-set '91–95%' as the default target on the neonatal oxygen prescription in EPMA and on the observation chart header, with standard monitor alarm limits agreed by the unit.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-181.csv`
- Pitfalls: Point-prevalence counts the same baby repeatedly → report both baby-days and unique babies.; Babies near term with different targets → agree the corrected age cut-off (for example until ROP risk ends) before cycle 1.; Alarm limits changed by staff for comfort → record but do not make them a pass criterion in cycle 1.; Auditor presence changes behaviour → vary the audit days and times.
- Pearls: Pair a trainee with a neonatal nurse champion for cot-side checks.; Photograph (without patient identifiers) examples of good charts for teaching.; Include ROP screening staff; they support tight targets.; Put the default in the EPMA build so it outlasts the rotation.

**NNA-182. What proportion of babies under 6 months with a first urinary tract infection that responds to treatment have a urinary tract ultrasound within 6 weeks?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG224 rec 1.3.2 (2022): "Send babies younger than 6 months with first-time UTI that responds to treatment for ultrasound within 6 weeks of the UTI, as outlined in table 4. [2007]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng224/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Renal tract ultrasound performed (not just requested) within 42 days of the positive urine sample; for atypical UTI, ultrasound during the acute illness.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive babies over the last 12 months (microbiology positive urine cultures filtered by age).
- Change: Add a 'UTI imaging per NICE table 4' section to the paediatric discharge letter with the scan requested before discharge and a named person to check the scan happened.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-182.csv`
- Pitfalls: Scan requested but family did not attend → count as a fail for the pathway but record DNA separately.; Culture-negative 'UTIs' treated empirically → include only culture-confirmed cases.; Radiology waiting list beyond 6 weeks → share data with radiology; the fix may be a booking slot.; Re-audit too early → allow 6 weeks follow-up after the last case.
- Pearls: Recruit a paediatric radiographer or sonographer lead.; Use microbiology reports to find cases rather than coding.; Also check DMSA and MCUG against tables 4–6 as secondary measures.; Build the imaging check into the virtual results clinic.

**NNA-183. What proportion of children presenting with a first suspected afebrile seizure have a 12-lead ECG recorded?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 1.2.2 (2022, updated 2026): "Evaluate people after a first suspected seizure with a 12-lead ECG to help identify cardiac-related conditions that could mimic an epileptic seizure. [2022]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/1-Diagnosis-and-assessment-of-epilepsy
- Pass: A 12-lead ECG recorded in ED, ward or first seizure clinic, with QTc documented, before or at the first specialist assessment.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive children over the last 6 months.
- Change: Add '12-lead ECG with QTc' as a mandatory item on the paediatric first-seizure proforma and the ED 'first seizure' triage order set.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-183.csv`
- Pitfalls: ECG done but not filed → search the ECG system as well as notes.; QTc not calculated by machine for young children → ask cardiology for a manual QTc protocol.; Febrile seizures included by mistake → apply the afebrile rule at case selection.; Clinic sees children already ECG'd in ED → count any ECG before clinic as a pass.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric epilepsy nurse and Epilepsy12 lead; ECG is also an Epilepsy12 measure.; Put an ECG machine in the PAU so it is not borrowed from adult ED.; Teach nurses to record ECGs in children at induction.; Report any abnormal QTc found as a case study.

**NNA-184. What proportion of children treated for convulsive status epilepticus leave hospital with an individualised emergency management plan?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 7.1.12 (2022, updated 2026): "After an episode of convulsive status epilepticus, agree an emergency management plan with the person if they do not already have one and there is concern that status epilepticus may recur. [2022]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/7-Treating-status-epilepticus-repeated-or-cluster-seizures-and-prolonged-seizures
- Pass: A written individualised emergency management plan (with rescue drug, dose and when to call 999) given to the family or confirmed already in place, documented by discharge; plus rescue medication prescribed.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 30 consecutive episodes over the last 12 months.
- Change: Create a standard paediatric emergency seizure plan template (buccal midazolam) in the EPR that auto-populates from the discharge prescription and prints with the discharge letter.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-184.csv`
- Pitfalls: Plan exists in the epilepsy nurse file but not the notes → check epilepsy nurse records before marking a fail.; First presentation with status where recurrence risk unclear → accept a documented decision that no plan is needed.; Small numbers → extend to 12 months and report counts.; Template not used by ward doctors → have the epilepsy nurse review all admissions weekly.
- Pearls: Co-lead with the paediatric epilepsy nurse specialist.; Use the regional or BPNA plan format families already know.; Check the rescue dose matches current weight.; Link plan issue to Epilepsy12 reporting.

**NNA-185. What proportion of children newly diagnosed with epilepsy have a documented discussion of epilepsy-related death risk, including SUDEP, with their parents or carers within 6 months of diagnosis?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG217 rec 10.1.4 (2022, updated 2026): "Discuss with people with epilepsy, and their families and carers if appropriate, their individual risk of epilepsy-related death, including SUDEP, from the time of diagnosis onwards. For young children, this discussion should be with the child's parents or carers. Discussion should include: supporting them to understand the risks of epilepsy-related death, including SUDEP exploring and agreeing ways to reduce the risks. [2022]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng217/chapter/10-Reducing-the-risk-of-epilepsy-related-death-including-sudden-unexpected-death-in-epilepsy
- Pass: Clinic letter or notes within 6 months of diagnosis record a discussion of SUDEP or epilepsy-related death and at least one risk-reduction measure.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive new diagnoses (Epilepsy12 case list).
- Change: Add a 'Safety and SUDEP discussed (date, measures agreed)' heading to the paediatric epilepsy clinic letter template, and include a SUDEP Action leaflet in the new diagnosis pack.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-185.csv`
- Pitfalls: Discussion held but not written down → the letter heading fixes this; do not accept verbal assurance in cycle 1.; Clinicians feel SUDEP talk is harmful at diagnosis → agree timing (within 6 months) and use NICE wording on individual risk.; Children with absence epilepsy only → keep them in; risk is low but discussion is still expected.; Letters dictated late → use clinic date, not letter date.
- Pearls: Invite the epilepsy nurse to lead the first discussion, with a leaflet.; Use a short script agreed by the team.; Track Epilepsy12 measures alongside so the change counts twice.; Place the heading in the shared template so all consultants use it.

**NNA-186. What proportion of girls on sodium valproate under paediatric care have a valproate Annual Risk Acknowledgement Form completed in the last 12 months?** (under-audited)
- Standard: MHRA Drug Safety Update, January 2024 (valproate regulatory measures; materials updated June 2025): "valproate must not be started in new patients (male or female) younger than 55 years, unless two specialists independently consider and document that there is no other effective or tolerated treatment, or there are compelling reasons that the reproductive risks do not apply ... at their next annual specialist review, women of childbearing potential and girls receiving valproate should be reviewed using the revised valproate Annual Risk Acknowledgement Form. A second specialist signature will be needed if the patient is to continue on valproate, however subsequent annual reviews will only require one specialist" https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-belvo-convulex-depakote-dyzantil-epilim-epilim-chrono-or-chronosphere-episenta-epival-and-syonellv-new-safety-and-educational-materials-to-support-regulatory-measures-in-men-and-women-under-55-years-of-age
- Pass: Current ARAF signed within the last 12 months and filed in the record (second specialist signature present where required).. Target: 100%. Sample: All eligible girls on the paediatric caseload (typically 10–30); census on one date.
- Change: Set up a paediatric valproate register held by the epilepsy nurse with ARAF due dates and an automatic clinic booking 1 month before expiry.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-186.csv`
- Pitfalls: Girls started by tertiary neurology → count them; the local team shares responsibility for the annual review.; Pre-pubertal girls seen as out of scope → MHRA covers girls; agree this before cycle 1.; Form completed but not scanned → check with the epilepsy nurse before marking a fail.; Second specialist unavailable → set up a buddy consultant for countersigning.
- Pearls: The register is the fix; the audit then becomes a quarterly check.; Pharmacy can supply the prescribing list in a day.; Include transition-age girls moving to adult services.; Share results with GP practices, which also hold ARAF duties.

**NNA-187. What proportion of children on intravenous maintenance fluids have plasma electrolytes and glucose measured at least every 24 hours?** (under-audited)
- Standard: NICE NG29 rec 1.2.4, 1.2.5 and 1.4.4 (2015, updated 2020): "Measure plasma electrolyte concentrations using laboratory tests when starting IV fluids, and then at least every 24 hours or more frequently if there are electrolyte disturbances. ... Measure blood glucose when starting IV fluids, and then at least every 24 hours or more frequently if there is a risk of hypoglycaemia. ... Measure plasma electrolyte concentrations and blood glucose when starting IV fluids for routine maintenance (except before most elective surgery), and at least every 24 hours thereafter." https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng29/chapter/Recommendations
- Pass: Electrolytes and glucose measured at the start of IV maintenance fluids and no gap longer than 24 hours between measurements while fluids continue.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive eligible children over the last 2 months.
- Change: Add a 'U&E and glucose due by: __' field to the paediatric IV fluid prescription (EPMA task or chart box) that must be completed each time fluids are re-prescribed.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-187.csv`
- Pitfalls: Surgical patients under a different team → include both medical and surgical wards; report separately.; Point-of-care gas sodium used instead of lab → NICE says laboratory tests; count gas as a fail in cycle 1 but record it.; Fluids running at low 'keep vein open' rates → exclude KVO fluids explicitly.; Blood draws are distressing → coordinate with other planned bloods; note this in feedback.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric pharmacist and the surgical ward sister.; Link to the NPSA hyponatraemia alert in teaching.; Report lowest sodium as a harm measure.; Put the field in the EPMA build so it outlasts the rotation.

**NNA-188. What proportion of children treated for suspected anaphylaxis are referred to a paediatric allergy service before discharge?** (new)
- Standard: NICE NG258 rec 1.1.12 (Referral to a specialist allergy service) and 1.1.15 (Discharge from A&E or inpatient care) (2026): "After emergency treatment for suspected anaphylaxis, offer the adult, young person or child a referral to a specialist allergy service (age-appropriate, where possible) consisting of healthcare professionals with the skills and competencies necessary to accurately investigate, diagnose, monitor and provide ongoing management of, and patient education about, anaphylaxis. [2011] ... At discharge following emergency treatment for suspected or known anaphylaxis, ensure the adult, young person or child has 2 in-date adrenaline auto-injectors (via a prescription, if needed) and knows when and how to use them, unless the anaphylaxis was due to a drug allergy and the drug can be easily avoided. [2011, amended 2026]" https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng258/chapter/Referral-to-a-specialist-allergy-service
- Pass: Referral to a paediatric allergy service sent (or documented as already under allergy care) by discharge.. Target: ≥95%. Sample: 40 consecutive episodes over the last 12 months (search ED coding and pharmacy adrenaline issue).
- Change: Add an 'anaphylaxis discharge bundle' to the ED/PAU EPR: a one-click paediatric allergy referral, two auto-injector TTO and a brand-specific training tick-box.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-188.csv`
- Pitfalls: Anaphylaxis coded as allergic reaction → search adrenaline administration records, not only diagnosis codes.; Referral left to GP → NICE says offer referral; accept a documented GP referral request only if agreed locally before cycle 1.; Drug-allergy cases where the drug is easy to avoid → auto-injector criterion does not apply; mark N/A.; Low numbers → extend window to 12 months.
- Pearls: Recruit the paediatric allergy nurse; they want the referrals.; Stock training pens in ED.; Include adolescents seen in adult ED.; Link the bundle to the Resuscitation Council anaphylaxis algorithm poster.

**NNA-189. What proportion of babies born to mothers with hepatitis B receive their first dose of hepatitis B vaccine within 24 hours of birth?** (new)
- Standard: UKHSA Green Book chapter 18, hepatitis B (updated February 2026): "Post-exposure immunisation is provided to infants born to women with hepatitis B infection, identified through antenatal screening, to prevent perinatal transmission at or around the time of birth. Immunisation of the infant should start as soon as possible after birth, no later than 24 hours, following the neonatal post exposure schedule." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/699c8d35e1c6bad1576fbc01/Green-book-on-immunisation-hepatitis-B-chapter_-_05022026.pdf
- Pass: Hepatitis B vaccine administration time ≤24 hours after birth; HBIG given with it where indicated.. Target: 100%. Sample: All eligible babies over the last 24 months (typically 15–40 in a DGH).
- Change: Add a hepatitis B birth-plan alert to the maternity EPR at booking that prints a prescription for the baby's vaccine (and HBIG if indicated) into the delivery pack.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-189.csv`
- Pitfalls: Small numbers → use 24 months and report each missed case.; HBIG ordered late from UKHSA → record HBIG order date; the Green Book allows HBIG up to 7 days but aims for the same time as vaccine.; Vaccine given but time not recorded → count as unknown, not a pass.; Mother's status unknown at delivery → include and record whether an urgent HBsAg test was done.
- Pearls: Co-lead with the antenatal screening coordinator.; Keep vaccine stock in the delivery suite fridge.; Notify GP and health visitor for the 4-week and 12-month doses and dried blood spot test.; Review with the UKHSA health protection team annually.

**NNA-190. What proportion of preterm babies still on the neonatal unit at 8 weeks of age receive their first routine immunisations within 7 days of the due date?** (new)
- Standard: UKHSA Green Book chapter 11, UK immunisation schedule (updated March 2026): "It is important that premature infants have their immunisations at the appropriate chronological age (counted from their date of birth), in accordance with the national routine immunisation schedule. As the benefit of vaccination is high in this group of infants, vaccination should not be withheld or delayed." https://assets.publishing.service.gov.uk/media/69cbb5ef8017673ffec0f313/Green-book-chapter-11-immunisation-schedules_30_03_26.pdf
- Pass: All 8-week vaccines (per the current schedule) given between day 56 and day 63 of chronological age, recorded in the red book and neonatal EPR.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: All eligible babies over the last 12 months (typically 20–40).
- Change: Add an automatic 'immunisations due' date to the BadgerNet daily summary and the ward-round board from admission, with pre-filled vaccine prescriptions ready on day 56.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-190.csv`
- Pitfalls: Unwell babies deferred appropriately → accept a documented clinical reason reviewed by a consultant as a pass; decide before cycle 1.; Vaccine stock not held on the unit → arrange pharmacy supply first.; Babies discharged just before day 56 → exclude; they fall to primary care.; Schedule changes (for example 2025–2026 changes) → use the schedule in force on the due date.
- Pearls: Recruit the neonatal discharge coordinator.; Put the due date on the cot card.; Tell parents the date at the 2-week update.; Check the red book is filled so the GP does not repeat doses.

**NNA-191. What proportion of term babies at risk of hypoglycaemia have their first blood glucose measured before the second feed, 2 to 4 hours after birth?** (under-audited)
- Standard: BAPM Framework for Practice: Identification and Management of Neonatal Hypoglycaemia in the Full Term Infant, Practice Point 15 (2024): "Measure the BG level before the second feed (2 to 4 hours after birth). If the baby shows feeding cues before 2 hours do not restrict feeding and allow baby to feed. Measure BG immediately if there are clinical signs suggestive of hypoglycaemia (Practice Point 2)." https://hubble-live-assets.s3.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/bapm/file_asset/file/2864/Identification_and_Management_of_Neonatal_Hypoglycaemia_Sept_24.pdf
- Pass: First blood glucose taken between 2 and 4 hours after birth (or earlier only if clinical signs documented).. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 40 consecutive at-risk babies over the last 4 weeks.
- Change: Add a 'first BG due at __:__ (birth + 2–4 h, before 2nd feed)' box to the NEWTT2 chart for at-risk babies, completed by the midwife at birth.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-191.csv`
- Pitfalls: Birth time and BG time from different systems with clock drift → check POCT meter clocks before cycle 1.; Risk factors missed at birth → report risk identification as a secondary item.; Babies tested at 1 hour 'to be safe' → share the framework rationale (normal physiological dip).; Midwife workload at night → keep the chart box simple.
- Pearls: Co-lead with a postnatal ward midwife and the infant feeding lead.; POCT middleware gives exact BG times; ask the POCT team for the download.; Pair with feeding support measures from the same framework.; Report term NNU admissions as the outcome that matters to managers.

**NNA-192. What proportion of children admitted with an acute medical problem are seen by a consultant paediatrician within 14 hours of admission?** (new)
- Standard: RCPCH Facing the Future: Standards for Acute General Paediatric Services, standard 2 (2015): "Every child who is admitted to a paediatric department with an acute medical problem is seen by a consultant paediatrician* within 14 hours of admission, with more immediate review as required according to illness severity or if a member staff is concerned." https://www.rcpch.ac.uk/resources/facing-future-standards-acute-general-paediatric-services
- Pass: First documented review by a consultant (or equivalent on the consultant rota) within 14 hours of the admission decision time.. Target: ≥90%. Sample: 50 consecutive admissions over 2 weeks, including weekends.
- Change: Add a 'consultant review: name, date, time' mandatory field to the paediatric post-take ward-round template and show 'awaiting consultant' flags on the electronic whiteboard.
- Template: `new_audits/templates/NNA-192.csv`
